\displaystyle\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)

\displaystyle\in[0\,,\;n]\ .

(41b)

The complexity relative entropy, like standard relative entropies, enjoys a scaling property in its second argument: for all
\lambda>0
,
\overline{D}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\lambda\Gamma)=\overline{D}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)-\log_{2}(\lambda)
.

By construction, the complexity (relative) entropy is not unitarily invariant.
On similar grounds, it does not satisfy a data-processing inequality.
Indeed, unitary evolution is a special case of data processing. Furthermore, a unitary can increase or decrease a pure state’s complexity.
Therefore, a unitary can increase or decrease a state’s complexity entropy.
Nevertheless, the complexity (relative) entropy monotonically increases (decreases) under partial traces.
Tracing out
k
qubits from
\rho
yields

\displaystyle\overline{D}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)

\displaystyle\geqslant\overline{D}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}\big(\tr_{k}({\rho})\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\tr_{k}({\Gamma})\big)\ ,

(42a)

\displaystyle\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)

\displaystyle\leqslant\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}\big(\tr_{k}({\rho})\big)+k\ .

(42b)

The complexity (relative) entropy is greater (less) than or equal to the hypothesis-testing (relative) entropy:
\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)\geqslant\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)
and
\overline{D}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)\leqslant\overline{D}_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)
.
In the large-
r
limit, one would expect the complexity (relative) entropy to converge to the hypothesis-testing (relative) entropy.
Yet, an exact convergence might not occur: some POVM effects might not be well approximated by any effect in
\mathsf{M}^{r}
.
This is due to our choice of
\mathsf{M}^{r}
and to the lack of ancillas in our computational model.
Nevertheless, we show in
Appendix

J
that, under certain conditions, the complexity (relative) entropy approximates the hypothesis-testing (relative) entropy up to constant error terms.
Alternatively, one might hope to ensure convergence to the hypothesis-testing (relative) entropy by allowing the use of one ancillary qubit in implementations of measurement effects, as introduced in Ref.
Brandao_21_Models
.

V.2
Bounds from well-known complexity measures

To what extent does the complexity entropy
\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\psi -NoValue-)
quantify the complexity of a pure state
\psi\equiv\lvert{\psi}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{\psi}\rvert
?
We bound the complexity entropy in terms of two complexity measures: the
strong complexity
of Ref.
Brandão et al. 2021
and the
approximate circuit complexity
(
Section

B.2
in
Appendix

B
).
We describe the bounds qualitatively here, deferring rigorous statements, and proofs thereof, to
Section

D.4
.

The complexity entropy reflects approximate circuit complexity as follows.
Assume that the set
\mathcal{G}
, used in the definition
(
11
)
, contains only unitary gates.
Consider beginning with some reference state, then applying gates in
\mathcal{G}
to prepare any state
\epsilon
-close to
\lvert{\psi}\rangle
in trace distance.
Let
C^{\epsilon}\left({\lvert{\psi}\rangle}\right)
denote the least number of gates in any such process.

C^{\epsilon}\left({\lvert{\psi}\rangle}\right)
is the
\epsilon
-approximate circuit complexity of
\lvert{\psi}\rangle
.
For all
r\geqslant C^{\epsilon}\left({\lvert{\psi}\rangle}\right)
,
\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{1-\epsilon^{2}}}-NoValue-(\psi -NoValue-)\approx 0
.
Furthermore,
\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{1-\epsilon^{2}}}-NoValue-(\psi -NoValue-)\not\approx 0
for all
r<C^{2\epsilon}\left({\lvert{\psi}\rangle}\right)
(see
Section

D.4
).
The complexity relative entropy also obeys an upper bound involving the
strong complexity
of Ref.
Brandão et al. 2021
(see
Section

D.4
).

V.3
Bounds from entanglement

We lower-bound an
n
-qubit state’s complexity entropy using a measure of the state’s entanglement.
Consider a 1D chain
S
of
n\geqslant 2
qubits:
S=S_{1}S_{2}\ldots S_{n}
.
Let
\rho
denote any state of
S
.
We define the following entanglement measure:

\displaystyle E(\rho)\coloneqq\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}I(S_{1}\ldots S_{j}\,:\,S_{j+1}\ldots S_{n})_{\rho}\ .

(43)

I(A\,:\,B)_{\rho}\coloneqq H(A)_{\rho}+H(B)_{\rho}-H(AB)_{\rho}
denotes the quantum mutual information defined in terms of the von Neumann entropy
H(X)_{\rho}\coloneqq-\tr\left({\rho\log\rho}\right)
.
The mutual information quantifies all correlations—classical and quantum—including correlations due to entanglement.

Assume that the operations
\mathcal{G}
in
(
11
)
are unitary gates that can act nontrivially only on two neighboring qubits (the gates are
geometrically local
).
We upper-bound the change in
E(\rho)
under one gate in
\mathcal{G}
.

Proposition V

(Change in
E(\rho)
under a two-qubit unitary)
.

Let
\rho
denote any quantum state of a 1D chain
S
of
n\geqslant 2
qubits.
Let
U
denote any unitary that can act nontrivially only on two neighboring qubits in
S
.
Then

\displaystyle\left\lvert{E\left({U\rho U^{\dagger}}\right)-E\left({\rho}\right)}\right\rvert\leqslant\frac{8\log(2)}{n-1}\ .

(44)

To prove
Section

V.3
, one observes that
U
can alter only one mutual information in
(
43
)
—the mutual information associated with (the bipartition that separates) the qubits on which
U
can act nontrivially.
In
Appendix

G
, we generalize
(
44
)
to account for the
potential entangling power

Eisert 2021
of
\mathcal{G}
.
This power quantifies the entanglement generable by one gate in
\mathcal{G}
.

By repeatedly applying
(
44
)
, we upper-bound the change in
E(\rho)
under
\leqslant r
gates in
\mathcal{G}
.
We then lower-bound the complexity entropy
H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)
in terms of
E(\rho)
as

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)\geqslant\frac{1}{\eta}\left[{E(\rho)-r\,\frac{8\log(2)}{n-1}+H(\rho)+\text{error terms}}\right]\ .

(45)

The error terms depend on
\eta
and
n
and vanish in the limit as
\eta\to 1
.
See
Appendix

G
for details.
In
Appendix

H
, we also investigate similar bounds that arise from natural dynamics under local Hamiltonians.
This setting is particularly relevant to locally interacting systems.

V.4
Evolution of the complexity entropy under random circuits

Consider an
n
-qubit circuit generated with gates sampled Haar-randomly from all two-qubit gates.
The circuit effects a unitary.
As the circuit depth grows, the unitary’s complexity grows, until saturating at a value exponential in
n
.
The complexity growth is at least sublinear
Brandão et al. 2021
and, for some nonrobust complexity measures
Haferkamp et al. 2022
;
Li 2022
;
Haferkamp 2023
, is linear.
We ask how well the complexity entropy tracks the complexity of a pure state evolving under a random circuit.

We consider
brickwork circuits

Haferkamp_21_Linear
;
Haferkamp 2022
.
A brickwork circuit consists of staggered layers of nearest-neighbor two-qubit gates.

The following proposition reveals the behaviors of the complexity entropy at a fixed complexity scale
r
: under short circuits, the complexity entropy vanishes.
Around a circuit depth
t
commensurate with
r
, the complexity entropy transitions to a near-maximal value (
Fig.

3
).
We bound the interval of
t
values in which the transition happens to values satisfying
t\sim r
.
We prove the proposition using a strategy borrowed from Ref.
Brandão et al. 2021
, which contains a similar proposition about the strong complexity.

Proposition VI

(Transition of the complexity entropy under random circuits)
.

Consider a depth-
t
, random
n
-qubit circuit
V
whose gates act in a brickwork layout.
Let
\lvert{\psi}\rangle\coloneqq V\lvert{0^{n}}\rangle
,
r\geqslant 0
, and
\eta\in(0,1]
.
If the circuit is short, such that
t\leqslant O(r)
, then

\displaystyle\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}\left(\lvert{\psi}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{\psi}\rvert\right)=0\ .

(46)

If the circuit is long, such that
t\geqslant\Omega(rn^{3})
, then

\displaystyle\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}\left(\lvert{\psi}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{\psi}\rvert\right)\geqslant n-\log_{2}\left({2/\eta}\right)\ ,

(47)

except with a probability
e^{-\Omega(n)}
over the sampling of
V
.

The proof of
(
46
)
is immediate: one can use the circuit
V
to construct a candidate
Q=V^{\dagger}\lvert{0^{n}}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{0^{n}}\rvert V\in\mathsf{M}^{r}
for the optimization
(
12
)
that defines
\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}\left(\lvert{\psi}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{\psi}\rvert\right)
.
The proof of
(
47
)
is presented in
Appendix

F
.

V.5
Data compression under complexity limitations

The complexity entropy naturally quantifies the optimal efficiency of data compression under computational limitations.
Let
\rho
denote any
n
-qubit state;
m
, a number of qubits; and
\epsilon\in[0,1]
, an error parameter.
To perform
data compression under complexity limitations
, one seeks a unitary
U
, composed from
\leqslant r
two-qubit gates, such that some subset
\mathcal{W}\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,n}\}
of
\lvert{\mathcal{W}}\rvert=m
qubits satisfies

\displaystyle F^{2}\bm{(}\tr_{\mathcal{W}}(U\rho U^{\dagger}),\lvert{0^{n-m}}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{0^{n-m}}\rvert\bm{)}\geqslant 1-\epsilon\ .

(48)

One
compresses

\rho
onto the qubits of
\mathcal{W}
, with an accuracy
1-\epsilon
and using
\leqslant r
gates.
For background, consider an agent who lacks complexity limitations (who operates in the limit as
r\to\infty
).
The hypothesis-testing entropy quantifies the optimal one-shot data-compression size of
\rho

Dupuis et al. 2013

\displaystyle m_{\mathrm{optimal}}\approx\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{1-\epsilon}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)\ .

(49)

Every protocol for data compression under complexity limitations can be mapped to a “simple” protocol for erasure.
The erasure protocol consists of computational gates followed by
reset
operations [see
Fig.

4
(b)].
The least number
m_{\mathrm{optimal},r}
of qubits onto which
\rho
can be compressed, with an accuracy
1-\epsilon
and using
\leqslant r
gates, satisfies

\displaystyle\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{1-\epsilon}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)-\log_{2}\left({1/\left[{1-\epsilon}\right]}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant m_{\mathrm{optimal},r}\leqslant\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{1-\epsilon}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)\ .

(50)

The complexity entropy
\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{1-\epsilon}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)
is defined with respect to the set
\mathcal{G}
of all two-qubit gates.
In
Appendix

K
, we prove
(
50
)
.

V.6
Complexity conditional entropy

In information theory, conditional-entropy measures quantify the randomness of a system
A
, as apparent to an observer given access to a system
B
that may share correlations with
A
.
The conditional entropy appears throughout classical and quantum information theory.
Applications include communication
Slepian and Wolf 1973
;
Abeyesinghe et al. 2009
;
Dupuis et al. 2014
, thermodynamic erasure with side information
del Rio et al. 2011
, and quantum entropic uncertainty relations
Coles et al. 2017
.
Consider defining a conditional entropy
\overline{H}_{*}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}B)_{\rho}
of a state
\rho_{AB}
.
A standard technique is to identify an appropriate relative entropy
\overline{D}_{*}
and to set
\overline{H}_{*}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}B)_{\rho}\coloneqq-\overline{D}_{*}(\rho_{AB}\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes\rho_{B})
.
Here,
\rho_{B}\coloneqq\tr_{A}({\rho_{AB}})
is the reduced state of
\rho
on
B

Tomamichel 2016
.

\overline{H}_{*}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}B)_{\rho}
reflects one’s ability to distinguish
\rho_{AB}
from a state that provides the same knowledge about
B
(
B
is in state
\rho_{B}
) but minimal knowledge about
A
(
A
is maximally mixed and uncorrelated with
B
).
In this interpretation,
\overline{D}_{*}
quantifies the notion of distinguishability.
Conditional entropies defined as above include the conditional min- and max-entropies and the conditional Rényi-
\alpha
entropies
Tomamichel 2016
.

We employ the complexity relative entropy
(
12
)
to define the complexity conditional entropy.
Denote any arbitrary bipartite state by
\rho_{AB}
.
For all
r\geqslant 0
and
\eta\in(0,1]
, the
complexity conditional entropy
of
\rho_{AB}
is

\displaystyle\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}B)_{\rho}\coloneqq-\overline{D}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(\rho_{AB}\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes\rho_{B})\ .

(51)

We prove the following properties of the complexity conditional entropy.
It, like the complexity entropy, decreases monotonically as
r
increases and increases monotonically as
\eta
increases.
Furthermore, the complexity conditional entropy is bounded in terms of the Hilbert-space dimensionality of
A
,
d_{A}
, as

\displaystyle-\log_{2}(d_{A})\leqslant\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}B)_{\rho}\leqslant\log_{2}(d_{A})\ .

(52)

The complexity conditional entropy also exhibits strong subadditivity: for every tripartite state
\rho_{ABC}
,

\displaystyle\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}BC)_{\rho}\leqslant\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}B)_{\rho}\ .

(53)

The strong subadditivity follows from the complexity relative entropy’s monotonicity under partial traces.
We prove
(
52
)
and
(
53
)
in
Section

D.6
.
The complexity conditional entropy has operational significance in a variant of the information-theoretic task of
decoupling
, we show next.

V.7
Decoupling from a reference system

First, we define the decoupling of a system
A
from a system
R
.
Define
d_{A}
as the Hilbert-space dimensionality of
A
and
\pi_{A}\coloneqq\mathds{1}_{A}/d_{A}
as a maximally mixed state.
We say that
A
is maximally mixed and decoupled from
R
if the joint state
\rho_{AR}
is the product
\pi_{A}\otimes\rho_{R}

Wilde 2013
.

Decoupling
A
from
R
means transforming a general
\rho_{AR}
into a state
\rho^{\prime}_{AR}
close to a state in which
A
is maximally mixed and decoupled from
R

Abeyesinghe et al. 2009
;
Dupuis et al. 2014
.
Decoupling is an information-theoretic primitive applied in randomness extraction
Renner 2005
;
Tomamichel et al. 2011
, quantum communication
Abeyesinghe et al. 2009
, and thermodynamic erasure with side information
del Rio et al. 2011
.

The standard information-theoretic task of decoupling can be defined as follows.
Alice possesses an
n
-qubit system
A
that might be correlated with a reference
R
.
The joint system begins in a state
\rho_{AR}
.
First, Alice applies to
A
a unitary
U_{0}
, preparing
\left({U_{0}\otimes\mathds{1}_{R}}\right)\rho_{AR}\left({U_{0}\otimes\mathds{1}_{R}}\right)^{\dagger}\eqqcolon\rho_{AR}^{\prime}
.
Then, Alice discards a subsystem
A_{1}
formed from
k\geqslant 0
qubits of
A
. A subsystem
A_{2}
, formed from
n-k
qubits, remains.
Alice sends
A_{2}
to a referee, who possesses
R
.
The referee attempts to distinguish the reduced state
\rho^{\prime}_{A_{2}R}
from
\pi_{A_{2}}\otimes\rho_{R}
.
Alice succeeds if the referee cannot distinguish the states to within some error tolerance.
Alice can ensure that
\rho_{A_{2}R}^{\prime}
approximates
\rho_{A_{2}}^{\prime}\otimes\rho_{R}
if
Dupuis et al. 2014

\displaystyle k\gtrsim\frac{1}{2}\left[{n-\overline{H}_{\mathrm{min}}^{\epsilon}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho}}\right]\ .

(54)

\epsilon>0
denotes a tolerance parameter.

\overline{H}_{\mathrm{min}}^{\epsilon}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho}
denotes the
smooth conditional min-entropy
of
\rho

Dupuis et al. 2014
;
Tomamichel 2016
.

Here, we introduce a complexity-constrained variant of decoupling.
Assume that
R
consists of qubits.
Assume further that both Alice and the referee can implement two-qubit gates that form a set universal for quantum computation.
We define our decoupling task as the task above, except we impose two complexity constraints.
First, we constrain Alice’s computational power, requiring that
U_{0}
be implementable with at most
r_{0}\geqslant 0
gates.
Second, we constrain the referee’s computational power.
Using at most
r_{1}\geqslant 0
gates, the referee performs a hypothesis test of the form described in
Section

II.3
, subject to type I and type II error constraints specified with
\eta\in(0,1]
and
\delta\in(0,1]
, respectively.
Alice succeeds in our decoupling task if there exists no hypothesis test by which the referee can correctly accept
\rho_{A_{2}R}^{\prime}
with a probability
\geqslant\eta
and incorrectly accept
\pi_{A_{2}}\otimes\rho_{R}
with a probability
\leqslant\delta
.
Equivalently, by
Section

II.3
, Alice is successful if

\displaystyle\overline{D}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r_{1}},\,{\eta}}\left(\rho^{\prime}_{A_{2}R}\mathclose{}\,\middle\|\,\mathopen{}\pi_{A_{2}}\otimes\rho_{R}\right)\leqslant-\log_{2}\left({\delta/\eta}\right)\ .

(55)

In terms of the complexity conditional entropy
\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r_{1}},\,{\eta}}(A_{2}\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho^{\prime}}
, the condition
(
55
)
is

\displaystyle n-k-\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r_{1}},\,{\eta}}(A_{2}\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho^{\prime}}\leqslant-\log_{2}\left({\delta/\eta}\right)\ .

(56)

Thus, Alice succeeds if
\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r_{1}},\,{\eta}}(A_{2}\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho^{\prime}}
is sufficiently close to its maximum value,
n-k
.

Fig. 5:
Decoupling with respect to a computationally bounded referee.
Alice’s system
A
might be correlated with a reference system
R
.
Alice applies to
A
a unitary
U_{0}
, then discards
k
qubits of
A
.
Alice retains an
(n-k)
-qubit system
A_{2}
.
Alice succeeds in our decoupling task if a referee cannot, using a circuit of
\leqslant r_{1}
gates, distinguish the final state of
A_{2}R
from
\pi_{A_{2}}\otimes\rho_{R}
.
The success condition depends on the conditional complexity entropy
\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r_{1}},\,{\eta}}(A_{2}\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho^{\prime}}
.
Conjecturing a property of that entropy, we bound, in terms of
\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho}
, the number of qubits that Alice can decouple from
R
.
Our bound mirrors the bound
(
54
)
, which holds in the absence of complexity constraints.

Before quantifying Alice’s decoupling capabilities, we posit two expectations.
First, we do not expect Alice’s complexity constraint to meaningfully change the number of qubits that she can decouple from
R
(unless
r_{0}
is very small).
Indeed, one may achieve near-optimal decoupling by choosing for
U_{0}
to be a random circuit of only
O\bm{(}n\log^{2}(n)\bm{)}
gates, in an all-to-all-coupling model
Brown and Fawzi 2015
.

In contrast, we expect the referee’s complexity constraint to substantially increase the number of qubits that Alice can decouple from
R
, by enabling Alice to succeed with strategies that would fail at standard decoupling.
For instance, consider a highly complex, maximally entangled state
\rho_{AR}
.
In the standard scenario, Alice cannot decouple any qubits from
R
.
[We can check this claim by substituting
\overline{H}_{\mathrm{min}}^{\epsilon}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho}=-n
in
(
54
)
.]
Now, suppose that the referee is computationally limited.
Alice’s system is apparently decoupled from
R
already: a complex state can be indistinguishable from a maximally mixed state, so the referee cannot distinguish
\rho_{AB}
from a decoupled state.
More precisely, suppose that
\rho_{AR}
is maximally entangled, that
\eta=1
, and that the referee has unlimited computational power.
Alice cannot decouple
n-k
qubits from
R
, if
\delta\leqslant 2^{-2(n-k)}
.

[
If Alice discards
k
qubits, then
\rho_{A_{2}R}^{\prime}
is a uniform mixture of
2^{k}
pure states.
In an optimal strategy, the referee transforms
\rho_{A_{2}R}^{\prime}
into
\rho_{A_{2}R}^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq\lvert{0^{2(n-k)}}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{0^{2(n-k)}}\rvert\otimes\left({\mathds{1}_{2}^{\otimes k}/2^{k}}\right)
, before implementing the measurement effect

Q\coloneqq\lvert{0^{2(n-k)}}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{0^{2(n-k)}}\rvert\otimes\mathds{1}_{2}^{\otimes k}\ .

(57)

Consequently, the referee can distinguish
\rho_{A_{2}R}^{\prime}
from
\pi_{A_{2}}\otimes\rho_{R}=\pi_{A_{2}R}
:
\overline{D}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r_{1}},\,{\eta}}(\rho^{\prime}_{A_{2}R}\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\pi_{A_{2}}\otimes\rho_{R})=-\log_{2}\bm{(}\tr({Q\pi_{A_{2}R}})/\tr({Q\rho_{A_{2}R}^{\prime\prime}})\bm{)}=2(n-k)\leqslant-\log_{2}\left({\delta/\eta}\right)
.
]

Now, however, suppose that the referee’s computational power is substantially limited and that
\rho_{AR}
is highly complex.
Alice may be able to decouple
n-k
(or more) qubits even if
\delta\leqslant 2^{-2(n-k)}
: the referee may require more computational power to perform a unitary necessary for distinguishing
\rho^{\prime}_{A_{2}R}
from
\pi_{A_{2}}\otimes\rho_{R}
.

How many qubits
k
must Alice discard to convince the referee that a maximally mixed, decoupled state was prepared?
We present a bound that generalizes
(
54
)
to our decoupling task.
The bound holds if one assumes that the complexity conditional entropy obeys an inequality reminiscent of the Rényi-entropy chain rule
Vitanov et al. 2013
;
Dupuis 2015
.

Conjecture VII

(Chain rule for the complexity conditional entropy)
.

Let
\rho_{ABR}
denote a quantum state of systems
A
,
B
, and
R
of
n_{A}
,
n_{B}
, and
n_{R}
qubits.
Let
r\geqslant 0
and
\eta\in(0,1]
.
We conjecture that

\displaystyle\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(B\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho}\leqslant\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(AB\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho}+n_{A}\ .

(58)

In words, introducing a system
A
can decrease the complexity conditional entropy by, at most, the size
n_{A}
of
A
.
The von Neumann conditional entropy
\overline{H}(B\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)
obeys an analogous bound, due to a chain rule and the lower bound
\overline{H}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}BR)\geqslant-n_{A}
:
\overline{H}(B\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)=\overline{H}(AB\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)-\overline{H}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}BR)\leqslant\overline{H}(AB\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)+n_{A}
.
This
\overline{H}(B\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)
bound reflects how introducing
A
can resolve only as much randomness as
A
can store.
Indeed, the bound saturates whenever
A
is maximally entangled with
B
(
R
).
In such a case,
B
and
R
are uncorrelated, and
A
purifies a maximally mixed state of
B
(
R
). Hence
A
resolves
n_{A}
maximally mixed qubits’ randomness.
Similar bounds follow from chain rules for Rényi entropies
Vitanov et al. 2013
;
Dupuis 2015
.

Using
Section

V.7
, we prove a bound on the number of qubits that Alice can decouple from
R
.

Theorem VIII

(Upper bound on the number of qubits that Alice can decouple)
.

Consider the complexity-restricted decoupling described above and depicted in
Fig.

5
.
Suppose
r_{1}\geqslant r_{0}
.
Assume
Section

V.7
.
Under the condition
(
55
)
(if Alice is successful), then

\displaystyle k\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\left[{n-\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r_{1}-r_{0}},\,{\eta}}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho}+\log_{2}\left({\delta/\eta}\right)}\right]\ .

(59)

We prove
Section

V.7
in
Appendix

L
.
The bound
(
59
)
mirrors the bound
(
54
)
, which governs decoupling without complexity restrictions.
Indeed, if
\eta\approx 0
, the conditional hypothesis-testing entropy approximates the smooth conditional min-entropy
Dupuis et al. 2013
.
As such, one can interpret the conditional complexity entropy, when
\eta\approx 0
, as a
complexity-aware version of the conditional min-entropy
.

The general achievability of the
bound
(
59
)
is unknown.
A standard technique for proving standard decoupling’s achievability involves
bounding the trace distance between a protocol’s output and a decoupled target, using the Hilbert–Schmidt norm
Abeyesinghe et al. 2009
;
Yard and Devetak 2009
;
Dupuis et al. 2014
.
One cannot readily extend this technique to
Section

V.7
: while the trace distance’s operational definition naturally extends to complexity-restricted POVM effects
Brandão et al. 2021
, such an extension is unknown for the Hilbert–Schmidt norm.

Section

V.7
appears to preclude a kind of pseudorandom quantum state that we call a
pseudomixed state
.
Let
A
and
B
denote systems of
n_{A}
and
n_{B}
qubits, respectively.
Let
\lvert{\chi}\rangle_{AB}
denote a pure state of
AB
.
Let
r\geqslant 0
and
\eta\in(0,1]
.

\lvert{\chi}\rangle_{AB}
is an
efficiently preparable pseudomixed state
on
B
if the complexity entropy of
\lvert{\chi}\rangle
is low,
\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\lvert{\chi}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{\chi}\rvert -NoValue-)\approx 0
, while the complexity entropy of the reduced state
\chi_{B}\coloneqq\tr_{A}\left({\lvert{\chi}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{\chi}\rvert}\right)
is high:

\displaystyle\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\chi_{B}-NoValue-)\geqslant\Omega\bm{(}\exp(n_{A})\bm{)}\ .

(60)

In other words,
B
appears exponentially more mixed without the purifying
A
.
The condition
(
60
)
directly contradicts the conjecture
(
58
)
.
Therefore, any efficiently preparable pseudomixed state would disprove
Section

V.7
.
We can therefore interpret
Section

V.7
as a
no-efficient-pseudomixedness conjecture
.

A naïve approach to efficiently constructing a pseudomixed state from
pseudorandom
states
Ji et al. 2018
;
Brakerski and Shmueli 2019
may
fail.
Standard constructions of pseudorandom states take as inputs
short, random bit strings
\kappa
.
The constructions yield efficient circuits for states
\lvert{\phi_{\kappa}}\rangle
that have the following property: no efficient quantum algorithm can distinguish
\poly(n)
copies of
\lvert{\phi_{\kappa}}\rangle
from the same number of copies of a Haar-random state.
One could naïvely expect to construct a pseudomixed state efficiently in the following way.
Consider an
n
-qubit system formed from two subsystems: an
O\bm{(}\log(n)\bm{)}
-qubit subsystem
A
and an
O(n)
-qubit subsystem
B
.
One can efficiently prepare the state

\lvert{\psi}\rangle_{AB}\coloneqq\sum_{\kappa}\lvert{\kappa}\rangle_{A}\otimes\lvert{\phi_{\kappa}}\rangle_{B}

(61)

from
\lvert{0^{n}}\rangle\,,

because pseudorandom states are efficiently preparable.
Let
r_{\mathrm{gen}}\leqslant\poly(n)
denote the number of gates required to prepare
\lvert{\psi}\rangle_{AB}
.
(One can generate
\lvert{\psi}\rangle_{AB}
by first preparing
A
in
\sum_{\kappa}\lvert{\kappa}\rangle_{A}
, by applying Hadamard gates.
Then, one implements a controlled unitary
\sum_{\kappa}\lvert{\kappa}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{\kappa}\rvert_{A}\otimes U_{\kappa}
.

U_{\kappa}
denotes an efficient unitary that prepares
\lvert{\phi_{\kappa}}\rangle
.)
For all
\eta
,
\overline{H}_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r_{\mathrm{gen}}},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\lvert{\psi}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{\psi}\rvert -NoValue-)=0
. Denote by
\psi_{B}\coloneqq\tr_{A}(\lvert{\psi}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{\psi}\rvert)
the reduced state of
B
. By discarding
A
, one forgets the seed
\kappa
used to construct the pseudorandom state
\lvert{\phi_{\kappa}}\rangle_{B}
. Therefore, no efficient algorithm can distinguish
\psi_{B}
from a Haar-random state.

\lvert{\psi}\rangle_{AB}
may appear to violate
Section

V.7
, looking highly mixed to a computationally limited observer.
Nevertheless,
\psi_{B}
may have a low complexity entropy, since the pseudorandom states have low complexities.
(
\psi_{B}
appears mixed to a computationally limited observer because the state’s preparation circuit, represented by the seed, is unknown.
Evaluating the complexity entropy, we imagine an observer who is given black-box access to a circuit that prepares the state.
Under such circumstances, pseudorandom states do not look random.
To appear highly mixed with respect to the complexity entropy, a pure state must have high complexity.)
Thus,
\lvert{\psi}\rangle_{AB}
does not necessarily constitute a counterexample to
Section

V.7
.

VI
Discussion

Our framework highlights quantum complexity in thermodynamics.
Thermodynamics is operational: it concerns how efficiently an agent, given certain resources, can accomplish certain tasks.
We incorporate into a version of the theory complexity restrictions on (i) the agent’s operations and (ii) the evaluation of the agent’s output.
This “agent” could be human, could consist of a system’s natural dynamics, etc.

Our framework introduces a new role for time in thermodynamics: a complex process requires a long time.
Conventional thermodynamics distinguishes only what is possible and impossible.
Augmented with complexity, our model of thermodynamics dictates what is practical and impractical.
A spin glass illustrates the need to incorporate complexity restrictions into thermodynamics: according to the conventional thermodynamic laws, a spin glass can cool to low-temperature states.
However, this cooling process would require a very long time, or a highly complex process.

We illustrated the interplay between complexity and conventional thermodynamics through Landauer erasure.
Consider aiming to erase a highly complex pure state.
One could “uncompute” the state, paying in complexity.
Alternatively, one could erase every qubit, paying work.
More generally, consider resetting an arbitrary state
\rho
with a success probability
\eta
.
The complexity entropy
H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)
quantifies the trade-off between the complexity and work costs.

Landauer erasure is one of many thermodynamic and information-processing tasks.
We analyzed data compression and decoupling, as well.
Many tasks merit analysis in the presence of complexity constraints.
We expect complexity-restricted entropies to quantify these tasks’ optimal efficiencies.

Our entropies quantify how mixed a state appears to a computationally limited agent.
For instance, even a pure state can have a high complexity entropy, if uncomputable by an agent.
Hence the complexity entropy quantifies a variation on pseudorandomness—more specifically, pseudomixedness: randomness apparent in determinstic phenomena, due to the observer’s computational limitations.

Unlike the standard hypothesis-testing entropy, the complexity entropy cannot be approximated via convex optimization.
We expect that computing the complexity entropy is typically hard, given strong evidence that computing the state complexity is hard
Gosset et al. 2014
;
van de Wetering and Amy 2023
.
Yet it might be possible to derive more-tractable bounds for the complexity entropy in specific settings.
Examples include low-complexity regimes as well as settings featuring random dynamics.
We derived such a bound for pure states evolving under random circuits (
Section

V.4
).

Other measures have been defined to quantify complexity or apparent randomness: the computational min-entropy
Chen et al. 2017
, the coarse-grained entropy
Gell-Mann and Hartle 2007
, the observational entropy
Šafránek et al. 2019
, the logical depth
Bennett 1988
, and the quantum Kolmogorov complexity
Berthiaume et al. 2001
;
Mora et al. 2006
.
These measures reflect different approaches to quantifying complexity, as illustrated in the introduction.
Rigorous comparisons between these quantities and the complexity entropies merit future work.

The main text illustrates the complexity entropies’ properties and usefulness; but generalizations are possible, and many are provided in
Appendix

D
.
For example, systems can be generalized beyond qubits to qudits.
We expect extensions to continuous-variable systems to be achievable.
Also, the complexity measure used can be replaced, as with Nielsen’s complexity
Nielsen 2005
;
Nielsen_06_Quantum
;
Nielsen et al. 2006b
;
Dowling and Nielsen 2008
.
One could even replace
r
with a matrix-product-state bond dimension—anything that constrains the POVM effects implementable.
One need only specify those POVM effects to construct general complexity entropies.

Another opportunity is to literally analyze—break apart—our
reset
and
extract
operations.
We have presented these operations as a computation’s basic units.
Yet each operation may consist of multiple steps, entailing extra costs.
To incorporate these costs into our proofs, one could apply results from Ref.
Taranto et al. 2023
.
Furthermore, we attributed to the
reset
the ideal work cost of
k_{\rm B}T\ln(2)
.
Any realistic
reset
will cost more work.
Such extra costs can be absorbed straightforwardly into our assumptions.
Alternatively, we could consider a different set of primitives
\mathcal{E}
that have fixed work costs
W(\mathcal{E})
and complexity costs
C(\mathcal{E})
.
(Any primitive operation could cost a nonzero amount of work and a nonzero amount of complexity.)
One possibility is to follow the approach in Ref.
Faist and Renner 2018
.
That is, allow as a primitive operation every completely positive, trace-preserving two-qubit map
\mathcal{E}
.
Then assign to
\mathcal{E}
a complexity cost of 1 and a work cost quantified by

\log\lVert{e^{\beta H/2}\mathcal{E}(e^{-\beta H})e^{\beta H/2}}\rVert
, wherein
H
denotes the operated-on system’s Hamiltonian.
Using this approach, one could lower-bound a process’s thermodynamic costs
Faist and Renner 2018
.

Complexity was recently incorporated into thermodynamics alternatively, in a resource theory
Yunger Halpern et al. 2022
.
In the resource theory of uncomplexity, an agent can perform arbitrarily many free operations.
Each operation is slightly noisy, and the agent will have a natural noise tolerance.
Hence the agent will naturally limit the number of operations they perform.
In the present paper, the agent’s complexity restriction is a hard, external constraint.
Just as complexity has recently emerged in resource theories, pseudorandomness has emerged in studies of quantum gravity
Kim et al. 2020
;
Bouland et al. 2019
;
Susskind 2020
.
We therefore anticipate the complexity entropy’s utility in understanding black holes in the context of the AdS/CFT correspondence, uniting tools of quantum information theory, high-energy physics, and statistical physics.

APPENDICES

Appendix A
Preliminaries, notation, and useful lemmas

Throughout these Appendices, we use the following definitions and conventions.
We denote the set of non-negative reals by
\mathbb{R}_{+}\coloneqq\{{x\in\mathbb{R}:x\geqslant 0}\}
.
We consider a system
S
equipped with a Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}_{S}
of a finite dimensionality
d_{S}
.
For any system
R
distinct from
S
, we denote the composition of
S
and
R
by
SR
.

\mathscr{H}_{S}
, as well as spaces of operators and of superoperators on
\mathscr{H}_{S}
,
are equipped with the standard topology of
\mathbb{C}^{d_{S}}
.
We denote the identity operator on
\mathscr{H}_{S}
by
\mathds{1}_{S}
.
We denote the identity superoperator on
\mathscr{H}_{S}
by
\operatorname{id}_{S}
.
We denote the single-qubit identity operator by
\mathds{1}_{2}
.
For any Hermitian operators
A
and
B
, we write
A\leqslant B
if
B-A
is positive-semidefinite.
A
subnormalized quantum state

\rho_{S}
is a positive-semidefinite operator that satisfies
\tr(\rho_{S})\leqslant 1
.
If
\tr(\rho_{S})=1
, then
\rho_{S}
is normalized and represents a physical quantum state.
In the absence of ambiguity, we omit system subscripts from operators.
We denote the projector onto a pure state
\lvert{\psi}\rangle
by
\psi\coloneqq\lvert{\psi}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{\psi}\rvert
.
We denote by
\pi_{S}\coloneqq\mathds{1}_{S}/d_{S}
the maximally mixed state of
S
.
For any sets
X
and
Y
of operators, we define
X\otimes Y\coloneqq\{A\otimes B:\,A\in X,\,B\in Y\}
.
An operator
A
has the operator norm
\lVert{A}\rVert
, defined as the greatest singular value of
A
.
The trace norm
\lVert{A}\rVert_{1}
is the sum of the singular values of
A
.
We denote by
\lvert{0}\rangle
the eigenvalue-1 eigenvector of the Pauli-
z
operator.
Tensor products are notated as
\lvert{0^{k}}\rangle\coloneqq\lvert{0}\rangle^{\otimes k}
.
A
POVM effect

Q
on a system
S
is a (Hermitian) positive-semidefinite operator satisfying
0\leqslant Q\leqslant\mathds{1}_{S}
.
Finally, all logarithms are base-
e
, unless otherwise indicated.

The
diamond norm
of a superoperator
\mathcal{X}
on
\mathscr{H}_{S}
is defined as

\displaystyle\lVert{\mathcal{X}}\rVert_{\diamond}\coloneqq\max_{\rho_{SR}}\left\{{\lVert{(\mathcal{X}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{R})(\rho_{SR})}\rVert_{1}}\right\}\ .

(62)

R
denotes any system isomorphic to
S
, and the maximization is defined with respect to all subnormalized states
\rho_{SR}
.
For any subnormalized state
\rho
on
S
, the diamond norm
\lVert{\mathcal{X}}\rVert_{\diamond}
upper-bounds the trace norm
\lVert{\mathcal{X}(\rho)}\rVert_{1}
as

\displaystyle\lVert{\mathcal{X}(\rho)}\rVert_{1}=\lVert{(\mathcal{X}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{R})(\rho_{S}\otimes\rho_{R})}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\lVert{\mathcal{X}}\rVert_{\diamond}\ .

(63)

For any completely positive, trace-preserving maps
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
, the diamond distance
\frac{1}{2}\lVert{\mathcal{E}-\mathcal{F}}\rVert_{\diamond}
quantifies the one-shot distinguishability of
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}

Wilde 2013
.

For any state
\rho
of
S
, the
von Neumann entropy
of
\rho
is
H(\rho)\equiv H(S)_{\rho}\coloneqq-\tr\bm{(}\rho\log(\rho)\bm{)}
.
For any positive-semidefinite operator
\Gamma
on
\mathscr{H}_{S}
, the (Umegaki)
quantum relative entropy
of
\rho
with respect to
\Gamma
is
D(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)\coloneqq\tr\bm{(}\rho[\log(\rho)-\log(\Gamma)]\bm{)}
.
If
S
is a joint system,
S=XY
, the
conditional quantum entropy

H(X\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}Y)_{\rho}
is
H(X\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}Y)_{\rho}\coloneqq H(XY)_{\rho}-H(Y)_{\rho}
.

The following inequality is used often in quantum information theory. The inequality is equivalent to a general lower bound on a version of the conditional min-entropy
Renner 2005
;
Tomamichel 2016
.

Lemma 1

(Partial order for joint states)
.

Let
X
and
Y
denote distinct quantum systems.
If
\rho_{XY}
denotes any subnormalized state on
XY
, then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d_{X}}\,\rho_{XY}\leqslant\mathds{1}_{X}\otimes\rho_{Y}\ .

(64)

Proof.

Let
C
denote a system isomorphic to
X
. Let
\{{\lvert{j}\rangle_{X}}\}
and
\{{\lvert{j}\rangle_{C}}\}
denote bases for the eigenspaces of
X
and
C
.
If
\lvert{\chi}\rangle_{C}\coloneqq\sum_{j}\lvert{j}\rangle_{C}/\sqrt{d_{X}}
, then
\rho_{XY}\otimes\chi_{C}\leqslant\rho_{XY}\otimes\mathds{1}_{C}
, since
\chi_{C}\leqslant\mathds{1}_{C}
.
Every positive map preserves the partial order on operators.
[If
\mathcal{N}
is a positive (linear) map acting on operators
A
and
B\geqslant A
, then
\mathcal{N}(B)\geqslant\mathcal{N}(A)
:
\mathcal{N}(B-A)=\mathcal{N}(B)-\mathcal{N}(A)
is positive-semidefinite, since
B-A
is positive-semidefinite.]
Hence, conjugating both sides of
\rho_{XY}\otimes\chi_{C}\leqslant\rho_{XY}\otimes\mathds{1}_{C}
by
\sum_{j}\lvert{j}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{j}\rvert_{X}\otimes\langle{j}\rvert_{C}
yields the pinching inequality

\displaystyle\rho_{XY}=d_{X}\left({\sum_{j}\lvert{j}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{j}\rvert_{X}\otimes\langle{j}\rvert_{C}}\right)(\rho_{XY}\otimes\chi_{C})\left({\sum_{k}\lvert{k}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{k}\rvert_{X}\otimes\lvert{k}\rangle_{C}}\right)\leqslant d_{X}\sum_{j}\lvert{j}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{j}\rvert_{X}\,\langle{j}\rvert_{X}\rho_{XY}\lvert{j}\rangle_{X}\ .

(65)

In terms of the Fourier-transformed states
\lvert{\tilde{l}}\rangle_{X}\coloneqq\sum_{j}e^{i2\pi lj/d_{X}}\lvert{j}\rangle_{X}/\sqrt{d_{X}}
, the pinching inequality is

\displaystyle\rho_{XY}\leqslant d_{X}\sum_{l}\lvert{\tilde{l}}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{\tilde{l}}\rvert_{X}\,\langle{\tilde{l}}\rvert_{X}\rho_{XY}\lvert{\tilde{l}}\rangle_{X}\ .

(66)

Applying the positive map
d_{X}\sum_{j}\lvert{j}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{j}\rvert_{X}\,\langle{j}\rvert_{X}(\cdot)\lvert{j}\rangle_{X}
to both sides of
(
66
)
yields

\displaystyle d_{X}\sum_{j}\lvert{j}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{j}\rvert_{X}\,\langle{j}\rvert_{X}\rho_{XY}\lvert{j}\rangle_{X}\leqslant d_{X}^{2}\sum_{jl}\lvert{j}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{j}\rvert_{X}\left\lvert{\langle{j}\mkern 1.5mu|\mkern 1.5mu{\tilde{l}}\rangle_{X}}\right\rvert^{2}\langle{\tilde{l}}\rvert_{X}\rho_{XY}\lvert{\tilde{l}}\rangle_{X}=d_{X}\mathds{1}_{X}\otimes\tr_{X}\left({\rho_{XY}}\right)=d_{X}\mathds{1}_{X}\otimes\rho_{Y}\ .

(67)

We have used
\left\lvert{\langle{j}\mkern 1.5mu|\mkern 1.5mu{\tilde{l}}\rangle_{X}}\right\rvert^{2}=1/d_{X}
.
Chaining together
(
65
)
and
(
67
)
proves the lemma.
∎

The following lemma provides a useful expression for, and bound on, the diamond distance between a unitary operation and the identity process.

Lemma 2

(Diamond distance between a unitary operation and the identity process)
.

Let
U
denote any unitary operator, and let
\mathcal{U}(\cdot)\coloneqq U({\cdot})U^{\dagger}
.
It holds that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\lVert{\mathcal{U}-\operatorname{id}}\rVert_{\diamond}

\displaystyle=\sin\left({\min\left\{{e(U),\frac{\pi}{2}}\right\}}\right),

(68)

where

\displaystyle e(U)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\min\left\{{\lVert{H}\rVert\,:\;H=H^{\dagger},\;\exists\chi\in\mathbb{R}\text{ such that }e^{-iH}=e^{-i\chi}U}\right\}\ .

(69)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\lVert{\mathcal{U}-\operatorname{id}}\rVert_{\diamond}\leqslant\lVert{U-\mathds{1}}\rVert\ .

(70)

The quantity
e(U)
is called the
potential entangling power
of
U
; a similar quantity is defined in Ref.
Eisert 2021
.

e(U)
is insensitive to global phases: for all
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
,
e(e^{i\chi}U)=e(U)
.

Inequality
(
70
)
automatically yields an upper bound on the diamond distance between arbitrary unitary operations.
For all unitaries
U
and
V
acting on the same Hilbert space, the operations
\mathcal{U}(\cdot)\coloneqq U({\cdot})U^{\dagger}
and
\mathcal{V}(\cdot)\coloneqq V({\cdot})V^{\dagger}
obey

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\lVert{\mathcal{U}-\mathcal{V}}\rVert_{\diamond}=\frac{1}{2}\lVert{\mathcal{V}^{\dagger}\mathcal{U}-\operatorname{id}}\rVert_{\diamond}\leqslant\lVert{V^{\dagger}U-\mathds{1}}\rVert=\lVert{U-V}\rVert\ .

(71)

The first (second) equality follows from the diamond (operator) norm’s invariance under unitary operations (operators).

Proof of
Appendix A
.

By Proposition 18 of Ref.
Nechita et al. 2018
,
\frac{1}{2}\lVert{\mathcal{U}-\operatorname{id}}\rVert_{\diamond}=\sin\left({\min\{{\alpha,\pi/2}\}}\right)
, wherein
\alpha
denotes half the angle of the shortest arc
A
, on the unit circle, that contains every eigenvalue (eigenphase) of
U
.
Let
\chi_{*}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
e^{i\chi_{*}}
is the midpoint of
A
.
(Since
U
has only finitely many eigenphases,
A
does not encompass the unit circle, so
A
has endpoints and a midpoint.)
By definition,
A=\left\{{e^{i\chi_{*}}e^{i\varphi}\,:\;\varphi\in[-\alpha,\alpha]}\right\}
.

A
has the endpoints
\theta_{\pm}\coloneqq e^{i\chi_{*}}e^{\pm i\alpha}
.

\theta_{\pm}
are eigenphases of
U
.
Otherwise, one could obtain an arc shorter than
A
that contains every eigenphase of
U
.

To prove
(
68
)
, we show that
e(U)=\alpha
.
Let
-iH_{*}
denote the unique logarithm of
e^{-i\chi_{*}}U
such that every eigenvalue of
H_{*}
lies in
(-\pi,\pi]
.
Since
H_{*}
is Hermitian and
e^{-iH_{*}}=e^{-i\chi_{*}}U
,
e(U)\leqslant\lVert{H_{*}}\rVert
.
Because every eigenvalue of
U
lies in
A
, every eigenvalue of
e^{-iH_{*}}
lies in the arc
A_{*}\coloneqq\left\{{e^{i\varphi}\,:\;\varphi\in[-\alpha,\alpha]}\right\}
, which one obtains by multiplying every point in
A
by
e^{-i\chi_{*}}
.
Consequently, every eigenvalue of
H_{*}
lies in
[-\alpha,\alpha]
, so
\lVert{H_{*}}\rVert\leqslant\alpha
.
Hence,
e(U)\leqslant\alpha
.

For the sake of contradiction, suppose
e(U)<\alpha
.
Let
H
denote any Hermitian operator that achieves the minimum in
(
69
)
.

e^{-iH}=e^{-i\chi}U
for some
\chi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\lVert{H}\rVert=e(U)<\alpha
.
Since every eigenvalue of
H
lies in
\left[{-\lVert{H}\rVert,\lVert{H}\rVert}\right]
, the arc
A_{H}\coloneqq\left\{{e^{i\chi_{*}}e^{i\varphi}\,:\;\varphi\in\left[{-\lVert{H}\rVert,\lVert{H}\rVert}\right]}\right\}
contains every eigenvalue of
e^{i\chi}e^{-iH}=U
.
Having the half-angle
\lVert{H}\rVert<\alpha
,
A_{H}
is shorter than
A
, a contradiction.
Therefore,
e(U)=\alpha
.

We now prove
(
70
)
.
Suppose
\alpha\geqslant\pi/2
.
Then
A
contains a half-circle and thus has an endpoint
\theta\in\left\{{\theta_{\pm}}\right\}
whose real part is nonpositive:
\phfqit@Realpart(\theta)\leqslant 0
.

\theta
is an eigenphase of
U
, so
\theta-1
is an eigenvalue of
U-\mathds{1}
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\lVert{\mathcal{U}-\operatorname{id}}\rVert_{\diamond}=\sin\left({\min\left\{{\alpha,\frac{\pi}{2}}\right\}}\right)=1\leqslant\left\lvert{\phfqit@Realpart(\theta)-1}\right\rvert=\left\lvert{\phfqit@Realpart(\theta-1)}\right\rvert\leqslant\left\lvert{\theta-1}\right\rvert\leqslant\lVert{U-\mathds{1}}\rVert\ .

Now, suppose
\alpha<\pi/2
.
Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\lVert{\mathcal{U}-\operatorname{id}}\rVert_{\diamond}

\displaystyle=\sin\left({\min\left\{{\alpha,\frac{\pi}{2}}\right\}}\right)=\sin\left({\alpha}\right)=\left\lvert{e^{i\chi_{*}}\sin\left({\alpha}\right)}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert{e^{i\chi_{*}}\left({e^{i\alpha}-e^{-i\alpha}}\right)}\right\rvert=\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert{\theta_{+}-\theta_{-}}\right\rvert=\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert{(\theta_{+}-1)-(\theta_{-}-1)}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert{\theta_{+}-1}\right\rvert+\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert{\theta_{-}-1}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant\lVert{U-\mathds{1}}\rVert\ .

(72)

The second inequality follows because
U-\mathds{1}
has the eigenvalues
\theta_{\pm}-1
.
∎

Appendix B
Measures of complexity and POVM-effect complexity

We define the complexity (relative) entropy in terms of POVM-effect complexity.
This section’s main purpose is to introduce general assumptions that define POVM-effect complexity.
Our setting’s generality enables one to define the complexity (relative) entropy for a range of physical systems, including qubits, qudits, and continuous-variable systems.

B.1
Complexity of superoperators and unitaries

Using conservative assumptions, we define complexity measures for superoperators and unitaries.
We later use these measures to construct measures of POVM-effect complexity.

Definition 3

(Superoperator-complexity measure)
.

Let
S
denote a composition of
N
quantum subsystems:
S=S_{1}S_{2}\ldots S_{N}
.
For every subset
I\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,N}\}
, let
S_{I}
denote the composition of the subsystems in
\{{S_{i}}\}_{i\in I}
.
A superoperator-complexity measure on
S
is a family of functions
C_{S_{I}}
that map completely positive, trace-nonincreasing maps to
\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{{\infty}\}
, for all
I\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,N}\}
.
The functions must have the following properties, for every
I\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,N}\}
:

1.

The identity operation has zero complexity:
C_{S_{I}}(\operatorname{id}_{S_{I}})=0
.

2.

No sequential composition of operations has a complexity that exceeds the sum of the individual operations’ complexities:
for any operations
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
on
S_{I}
,
C_{S_{I}}(\mathcal{E}_{2}\mathcal{E}_{1})\leqslant C_{S_{I}}(\mathcal{E}_{1})+C_{S_{I}}(\mathcal{E}_{2})
.

3.

No parallel composition of operations has a complexity that exceeds the sum of the individual operations’ complexities:
for any
J
disjoint from
I
, for any operation
\mathcal{E}_{1}
on
S_{I}
, and for any operation
\mathcal{E}_{2}
on
S_{J}
,
C_{S_{I}S_{J}}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{2})\leqslant C_{S_{I}}(\mathcal{E}_{1})+C_{S_{J}}(\mathcal{E}_{2})
.

For every operation
\mathcal{E}
on any
S_{I}
, we employ the shorthand notation
C_{S}(\mathcal{E})\coloneqq C_{S_{I}}(\mathcal{E})
.
Accordingly, we denote a superoperator-complexity measure by its function
C_{S}
, for convenience.

The following is a natural way to construct a superoperator-complexity measure:
Choose a set
\mathcal{G}
of elementary operations.
Define the complexity of an arbitrary operation
\mathcal{E}
as the minimum number of operations in
\mathcal{G}
that, under composition, effect
\mathcal{E}
.

Definition 4

(Circuit-superoperator-complexity measure)
.

Let
S=S_{1}S_{2}\ldots S_{N}
denote a quantum system.
We employ the notation of
Section

B.1
.
For every subset
I\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,N}\}
, let
\mathcal{G}_{S_{I}}
denote an (arbitrary) set of completely positive, trace-nonincreasing maps such that
\operatorname{id}_{S_{I}}\in\mathcal{G}_{S_{I}}
and
\mathcal{G}_{S_{I}}\subset\mathcal{G}_{S_{J}}
for all
J\supset I
.
Denote the set
\mathcal{G}_{S}
by
\mathcal{G}
.
A
circuit-superoperator-complexity measure
on
S
associated with
\mathcal{G}
is a family of functions
C_{\mathcal{G},S_{I}}
, for any
I\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,N}\}
, defined as

\displaystyle C_{\mathcal{G},S_{I}}(\mathcal{E})\coloneqq\min\left\{{r\in\mathbb{N}:\mathcal{E}_{r}\cdots\mathcal{E}_{2}\mathcal{E}_{1}=\mathcal{E}\,,\;\mathcal{E}_{i}\in\mathcal{G}_{S_{I}}\>\,\forall i\in\left\{{1,2,\ldots,r}\right\}}\right\}\ .

(73)

We introduce three conventions.
First, we denote a circuit-superoperator-complexity measure by its function
C_{\mathcal{G},S}
.
Second, we designate the composition of a zero number of operations on
S_{I}
as the identity operation
\operatorname{id}_{S_{I}}
.
Third, we set
C_{S_{I}}(\mathcal{E})=\infty
whenever no finite sequence of operations from
\mathcal{G}_{S_{I}}
implements an operation
\mathcal{E}
exactly.

The following proposition confirms that every circuit-complexity measure (
Section

B.1
) is a superoperator-complexity measure (
Section

B.1
).

Proposition 5

(Compatibility of circuit-superoperator complexity and general-superoperator complexity)
.

Define
S
and the gate set
\mathcal{G}
as in
Section

B.1
.
Every circuit-superoperator-complexity measure on
S
associated with
\mathcal{G}
is a superoperator-complexity measure.

Proof.

We verify that every circuit-superoperator-complexity measure
C_{\mathcal{G},S}
has the three properties in
Section

B.1
.
Property
1
holds because, by definition,
\operatorname{id}_{S_{I}}\in\mathcal{G}_{S_{I}}
; and, by convention,
\operatorname{id}_{S_{I}}
is the sequential composition of zero operations.
Property
2
automatically characterizes every sequential composition that involves an infinite-complexity operation.
Now, we prove that property
2
characterizes finite-complexity operations
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
on
S_{I}
.
If the operations’ complexities are
C_{S_{I}}(\mathcal{E})=r
and
C_{S_{I}}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})=r^{\prime}
, we can decompose
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
as sequences of
r
and
r^{\prime}
operations in
\mathcal{G}_{S_{I}}
, respectively.
Therefore, we can decompose
\mathcal{E^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}
as a sequence of
r+r^{\prime}
operations in
\mathcal{G}_{S_{I}}
.
Hence
C_{S_{I}}(\mathcal{E^{\prime}}\mathcal{E})\leqslant r+r^{\prime}=C_{S_{I}}(\mathcal{E})+C_{S_{I}}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
.
Property
3
follows as a special case of Property
2
: one can express every operation
\mathcal{E}
on
S_{I}
as the operation
\mathcal{E}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{S_{J}}
on
S_{I}S_{J}
and vice versa.
∎

Many complexity measures in the literature are defined only for unitary operations.
The following definition specializes
Section

B.1
to such situations.

Definition 6

(Unitary-complexity measure)
.

A
unitary-complexity measure
is
a superoperator-complexity measure that assigns finite values only to unitary operations.
A unitary-complexity measure
C_{S}
is called
adjoint-invariant
if
C_{S}(\mathcal{U})=C_{S}(\mathcal{U}^{\dagger})
for every unitary operation
\mathcal{U}
on
S
.

Let
U
denote any unitary operator on
\mathscr{H}_{S}
, and let
\mathcal{U}(\cdot)\coloneqq U(\cdot)U^{\dagger}
.
We write
C_{S}(U)
as a shorthand for
C_{S}(\mathcal{U})
, for convenience.
Correspondingly, a
unitary-circuit-complexity measure
is a unitary-complexity measure that is also a circuit-complexity measure.
Equivalently, a unitary-circuit-complexity measure is a circuit-complexity measure associated with a set of unitary gates.

B.2
Quantum state complexity

Here, we introduce state-complexity measures in general, for completeness and for use in later appendices.

Definition 7

(Exact-state-complexity measure)
.

Let
S
denote a quantum system.
Let
C_{S}
denote any superoperator-complexity measure; and
\rho_{0}
, any state of
S
.
The
exact-state-complexity measure
with respect to
C_{S}
and
\rho_{0}
is

\displaystyle C_{S}^{\rho_{0}}(\rho)\coloneqq\inf\left\{{C_{S}(\mathcal{E}):\mathcal{E}(\rho_{0})=\rho}\right\}\ .

(74)

An exact-state-complexity measure
C_{S}^{\rho_{0}}
is called an
exact-pure-state-complexity measure
if
\rho_{0}
is pure and if
C_{S}^{\rho_{0}}
assigns finite values only to pure states.

C_{S}^{\rho_{0}}
is such a measure if, for instance,
\rho_{0}
is pure and
C_{S}
is a unitary-complexity measure.

Definition 8

(Approximate-state-complexity measure)
.

Let
S
denote a quantum system.
Let
C_{S}
denote any superoperator-complexity measure; and
\rho_{0}
, any state of
S
.
Let
\delta\geqslant 0
.
The
\delta
-approximate-state-complexity measure
with respect to
C_{S}
and
\rho_{0}
is

\displaystyle C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\,\delta}(\rho)\coloneqq\lim_{\zeta\to 0^{+}}\inf\left\{{C_{S}(\mathcal{E})\,:\;\frac{1}{2}\lVert{\mathcal{E}(\rho_{0})-\rho}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\delta+\zeta}\right\}\ .

(75)

The stabilization
\lim_{\zeta\to 0^{+}}
in
(
75
)
helps smooth
C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\,\delta}
at unusual discontinuities in superoperator-complexity measures.
For instance, consider any set
\mathcal{G}_{0}
of two-qubit unitary gates that is universal for quantum computation.
Let
\mathcal{G}
denote the set of
n
-qubit unitaries realizable as finite sequences of gates in
\mathcal{G}_{0}
.

\mathcal{G}
is closed under composition.
Let
C_{\mathcal{G}}
denote the unitary-circuit-complexity measure associated with
\mathcal{G}
(
Section

B.1
).
By construction, the identity
\mathds{1}_{2}^{\otimes n}
has zero complexity, and every unitary
U\in\mathcal{G}
has unit complexity:
C_{\mathcal{G}}(U)=1
.
Any other unitary has infinite complexity, since it can only be approximated by a sequence of gates in
\mathcal{G}_{0}
—and thus by a unitary in
\mathcal{G}

BookNielsenChuang2000
.
Let
\lvert{\psi_{0}}\rangle
denote any pure
n
-qubit state.
The stabilization ensures that
C_{S}^{\psi_{0},\,0}(\psi)=1
for all pure states
\lvert{\psi}\rangle\neq\lvert{\psi_{0}}\rangle
.
Without the stabilization, every state
\lvert{\psi}\rangle
inaccessible from
\lvert{\psi_{0}}\rangle
by any unitary in
\mathcal{G}
would have an infinite complexity:
C_{S}^{\psi_{0},\,0}(\psi)=\infty
.

As an immediate consequence of
(
75
)
,
C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\delta}
is right-continuous in
\delta
:

\displaystyle\lim_{\zeta\to 0^{+}}C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\delta+\zeta}(\rho)=C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\delta}(\rho)\ .

(76)

An approximate-state-complexity measure
C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\,\delta}
is called an
approximate-pure-state-complexity measure
if
\rho_{0}
is pure and if
C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\,0}
assigns finite values only to pure states.

C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\,\delta}
is such a measure if, for instance,
\rho_{0}
is pure and
C_{S}
is a unitary-complexity measure.

The following proposition provides an alternative expression for
(
75
)
.
From this expression, we derive a condition under which the stabilization in
(
75
)
becomes inconsequential.

Proposition 9

(Alternative expression for approximate-state-complexity measure)
.

Let
S
denote a quantum system.
Let
C_{S}
denote any superoperator-complexity measure; and
\rho_{0}
, any state of
S
.
Let
\delta\geqslant 0
.
It holds that

\displaystyle C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\delta}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{{r\,:\;\dist_{1}(\rho,\mathfrak{G}_{r}[\rho_{0}])\leqslant\delta}\right\}\ ,

(77)

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{r}[\rho_{0}]\coloneqq\left\{{\mathcal{E}(\rho_{0})\,:\;C_{S}(\mathcal{E})\leqslant r}\right\}\ \;\;\text{and}\;\;\dist_{1}(\rho,T)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf_{\rho^{\prime}\in T}\left\{{\frac{1}{2}\big\lVert{\rho-\rho^{\prime}}\big\rVert_{1}}\right\}\ ,

(78)

and wherein
T
denotes any set of states.

We now show that, under simple conditions, we can forgo the stabilization in the definition of the approximate-state-complexity measure.

Corollary 10

(No stabilization of the approximate-state-complexity measure required for compact gate sets)
.

Consider the setting in
Section

B.2
.
Suppose that, for all
r\geqslant 0
, the sets
\mathfrak{G}_{r}[\rho_{0}]
are compact.
Then

\displaystyle C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\,\delta}(\rho)=\inf\left\{{C_{S}(\mathcal{E})\,:\;\frac{1}{2}\lVert{\mathcal{E}(\rho_{0})-\rho}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\delta}\right\}\ .

(79)

Furthermore, the sets
\mathfrak{G}_{r}[\rho_{0}]
are compact if
C_{S}
is a circuit-complexity measure (
Section

B.1
) associated with a compact gate set
\mathcal{G}_{S}
.

Proof of
subsection B.2
.

Let
\rho
denote any state of
S
.
We show that the expressions in
(
75
)
and
(
77
)
equal

\displaystyle r^{*}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf\left\{{r\ :\ \forall\ \zeta>0\ ,\ \exists\ \mathcal{E}\ \text{ such that }\ C_{S}(\mathcal{E})\leqslant r\ \text{ and }\ \frac{1}{2}\lVert{\mathcal{E}(\rho_{0})-\rho}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\delta+\zeta}\right\}\ .

(80)

The infimum in
(
77
)
equals
r^{*}
, since

\displaystyle\inf\left\{{r\ :\ \dist_{1}\big({\rho,\mathfrak{G}_{r}[\rho_{0}]}\big)\leqslant\delta}\right\}

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{{r\,:\ \forall\ \zeta>0\;,\ \exists\ \rho^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{G}_{r}[\rho_{0}]\;\text{ such that }\ \frac{1}{2}\lVert{\rho-\rho^{\prime}}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\delta+\zeta}\right\}=r^{*}\ .

(81)

Now, consider
C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\delta}(\rho)
, as defined in
(
75
)
.
We show that
C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\delta}(\rho)=r^{*}
by showing first that
r^{*}\leqslant C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\delta}(\rho)
and then that
C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\delta}(\rho)\leqslant r^{*}
.
Consider any
\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}>0
.
By
(
75
)
, there exists an
\mathcal{E}
such that
C_{S}(\mathcal{E})\leqslant C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\delta}(\rho)+\zeta^{\prime}
and
\frac{1}{2}\lVert{\mathcal{E}(\rho_{0})-\rho}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\delta+\zeta
.
Hence,
r=C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\delta}(\rho)+\zeta^{\prime}
is a candidate for the optimization in
(
80
)
, so

\displaystyle r^{*}\leqslant C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\delta}(\rho)+\zeta^{\prime}\ .

(82)

Thus,
r^{*}\leqslant C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\delta}(\rho)
, since
\zeta^{\prime}>0
is arbitrary.
By the infimum in
(
80
)
, there exists an
\mathcal{E}
and an
r\leqslant r^{*}+\zeta^{\prime}
such that
C_{S}(\mathcal{E})\leqslant r
and
\frac{1}{2}\lVert{\mathcal{E}(\rho_{0})-\rho}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\delta+\zeta
.
Hence,
r
is lower-bounded as

\displaystyle\inf\left\{{C_{S}(\mathcal{E})\ :\ \frac{1}{2}\lVert{\mathcal{E}(\rho_{0})-\rho}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\delta+\zeta}\right\}\leqslant r\leqslant r^{*}+\zeta^{\prime}\ .

(83)

Thus, the infimum in
(
83
)
lower-bounds
r^{*}
, since
\zeta^{\prime}>0
is arbitrary.
Consequently,

\displaystyle C_{S}^{\rho_{0},\delta}(\rho)=\lim_{\zeta\to 0^{+}}\inf\left\{{C_{S}(\mathcal{E})\ :\ \frac{1}{2}\lVert{\mathcal{E}(\rho_{0})-\rho}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\delta+\zeta}\right\}\leqslant r^{*}\ .

(84)

∎

Proof of
subsection B.2
.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{G}_{r}[\rho_{0}]
is compact for all
r\geqslant 0
.
The infimum defining
\dist_{1}({\rho,\mathfrak{G}_{r}[\rho_{0}]})
, in
(
78
)
, is achieved by some state in
\mathfrak{G}_{r}[\rho_{0}]
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\dist_{1}\left({\rho,\mathfrak{G}_{r}[\rho_{0}]}\right)=\min\left\{{\frac{1}{2}\lVert{\mathcal{E}(\rho_{0})-\rho}\rVert_{1}\ :\ C_{S}(\mathcal{E})\leqslant r}\right\}\ .

(85)

Substituting
(
85
)
into
(
77
)
yields
(
79
)
.

Now, suppose that
C_{S}
is a circuit-complexity measure (
Section

B.1
) associated with a gate set
\mathcal{G}_{S}
.
Assume that
\mathcal{G}_{S_{I}}
is compact for all
I\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,N}\}
.
Consider the function
F
that maps every sequence
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{r})
of gates in
\mathcal{G}_{S}
to an action on
\rho_{0}
, as follows:

\displaystyle F\left({\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{r}}\right)\coloneqq\left({\mathcal{E}_{r}\cdots\mathcal{E}_{2}\mathcal{E}_{1}}\right)\left({\rho_{0}}\right)\ .

(86)

By definition,
F
is a map from
(\mathcal{G}_{S})^{\times r}
to
\mathfrak{G}_{r}[\rho_{0}]
.

F
is continuous, since it is a polynomial map.

(\mathcal{G}_{S})^{\times r}
is compact, since the finite product of compact sets is compact.
Therefore,
\mathfrak{G}_{r}[\rho_{0}]
is compact, since continuous functions preserve compactness.
∎

B.3
POVM-effect complexity

A central ingredient in the complexity relative entropy’s definition is a family
\{\mathsf{M}^{r}\}
of sets of POVM effects.

\{\mathsf{M}^{r}\}
is a formal parameter that can accommodate any notion of POVM-effect complexity.
Each set
\mathsf{M}^{r}
contains precisely the POVM effects of complexities
\leqslant r
.
An example of such a family of sets is defined via
(
11
)
.

Here, we present conservative assumptions about every family
\{\mathsf{M}^{r}\}
to ensure that it has properties necessary for our construction of the complexity (relative) entropy.

Definition 11

(POVM-effect-complexity sets)
.

Let
S
denote a composition of
N
quantum subsystems:
S=S_{1}S_{2}\ldots S_{N}
.
For every subset
I\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,N}\}
, let
S_{I}
denote the composition of the subsystems in
\{{S_{i}}\}_{i\in I}
.
A
family of POVM-effect-complexity sets
on
\mathscr{H}_{S}
is a family
\{{\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S_{I}}}\}_{r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
of sets of POVM effects, for each
S_{I}
.
Each
\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S_{I}}
is a
POVM-effect-complexity set
.
The family must satisfy the following axioms, for every
I\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,N}\}
:

(i)

The identity operator has zero complexity:
\mathds{1}_{S_{I}}\in\mathsf{M}^{0}_{S_{I}}
.

(ii)

For all
r^{\prime}\geqslant r\geqslant 0
,
\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S_{I}}\subset\mathsf{M}^{r^{\prime}}_{S_{I}}
.

(iii)

For all
r,r^{\prime}\geqslant 0
and for all
J
disjoint from
I
,
\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S_{I}}\otimes\mathsf{M}^{r^{\prime}}_{S_{J}}\subset\mathsf{M}^{r+r^{\prime}}_{S_{I}S_{J}}
.

For convenience, we write
\{{\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S}}\}
as shorthand for a family
\{{\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S_{I}}}\}_{r,I}
.

B.4
POVM-effect-complexity sets from simple POVM effects and a superoperator-complexity measure

A natural way to construct POVM-effect-complexity sets is as follows:
Designate a set of “simple,” zero-complexity POVM effects.
Define the complexity of an effect
Q
as the minimum complexity of any superoperator that maps a simple effect to
Q
.
In the main text, we use this procedure to construct the sets
\mathsf{M}^{r}
in
(
11
)
from single-qubit projectors and a circuit-complexity measure.

The conditions in
Section

B.3
imply that zero-complexity effects must satisfy two conditions:
(i) the identity operator is a zero-complexity effect.
(ii) The parallel composition of two zero-complexity effects is a zero-complexity effect.
Any set of “simple” POVM effects must satisfy these criteria, which we formalize in the following definition.

Definition 12

(Simple POVM effects)
.

Let
S
denote a composition of
N
quantum subsystems:
S=S_{1}S_{2}\ldots S_{N}
.
For every subset
I\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,N}\}
, let
S_{I}
denote the composition of the subsystems in
\{{S_{i}}\}_{i\in I}
.
Let
\{\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}}\}
denote a family of sets of POVM effects.
The
\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}}
are sets of simple POVM effects if, for all
I\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,N}\}
, the following conditions hold:

1.

\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}}
contains the identity operator:
\mathds{1}_{S_{I}}\in\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}}
.

2.

\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}}\otimes\mathsf{P}_{S_{J}}\subset\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}S_{J}}
for all
J
disjoint from
I
.

For convenience, we denote a family
\{{\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}}}\}
by its set
\mathsf{P}_{S}
.

We now construct POVM-effect-complexity sets from simple POVM effects and a superoperator-complexity measure.

Definition 13

(POVM-effect-complexity sets from simple effects and a superoperator-complexity measure)
.

Let
S=S_{1}S_{2}\ldots S_{N}
denote a quantum system as in
Section

B.4
.
Let
\mathsf{P}_{S}
denote any set of simple POVM effects (
Section

B.4
); and
C_{S}
, any superoperator-complexity measure (
Section

B.1
).
For all
r\geqslant 0
and
I\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,N}\}
, we define

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S_{I}}\left({\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}},C_{S_{I}}}\right)\coloneqq\left\{{\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(P)\,:\;P\in\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}}\,,\;C_{S_{I}}(\mathcal{E})\leqslant r}\right\}\ .

(87)

Proposition 14
.

The sets
\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S_{I}}\left({\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}},C_{S_{I}}}\right)
constructed in
(
87
)
define a family of POVM-effect-complexity sets (
Section

B.3
).

Proof.

We write
\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S_{I}}=\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S_{I}}\left({\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}},C_{S_{I}}}\right)
for short.
We verify that the sets
\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S_{I}}
have the three properties in
Section

B.3
.
Property
(i)
holds because
\mathds{1}_{S_{I}}=\operatorname{id}_{S_{I}}^{\dagger}\left({\mathds{1}_{S_{I}}}\right)
,
\mathds{1}_{S_{I}}\in\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}}
(
Section

B.4
), and
C_{S_{I}}\left({\operatorname{id}_{S_{I}}}\right)=0
(
Section

B.1
).
Property
(ii)
holds because, for all
r^{\prime}\geqslant r\geqslant 0
and for all operations
\mathcal{E}
on
S_{I}
,
C_{S_{I}}(\mathcal{E})\leqslant r
implies
C_{S_{I}}(\mathcal{E})\leqslant r^{\prime}
.
To show that Property
(iii)
holds, we consider any POVM effects
Q_{1}\in\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S_{I}}
and
Q_{2}\in\mathsf{M}^{r^{\prime}}_{S_{J}}
, with
I
and
J
disjoint.

Q_{1}=\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\dagger}(P_{1})
for some effect
P_{1}\in\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}}
and for some operation
\mathcal{E}_{1}
satisfying
C_{S_{I}}(\mathcal{E}_{1})\leqslant r
.
Similarly,
Q_{2}=\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\dagger}(P_{2})
for some effect
P_{2}\in\mathsf{P}_{S_{J}}
and for some operation
\mathcal{E}_{2}
satisfying
C_{S_{J}}(\mathcal{E}_{2})\leqslant r^{\prime}
.
By
Section

B.4
,
P_{1}\otimes P_{2}\in\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}S_{J}}
.
By
Section

B.1
,
C_{S_{I}S_{J}}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{2})\leqslant C_{S_{I}}(\mathcal{E}_{1})+C_{S_{J}}(\mathcal{E}_{2})\leqslant r+r^{\prime}
.
Thus,
Q_{1}\otimes Q_{2}=\big({\mathcal{E}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{2}}\big)^{\dagger}\big({P_{1}\otimes P_{2}}\big)\in\mathsf{M}^{r+r^{\prime}}_{S_{I}S_{J}}
.
∎

By
(
87
)
, every simple POVM effect is a zero-complexity effect, since the identity process has zero complexity.
In general, the converse is false: not every zero-complexity effect is a simple POVM effect.
Indeed, a nontrivial zero-complexity operation applied to a simple effect may yield a zero-complexity effect that is not a simple effect.
Let
\mathsf{P}
denote a set of simple POVM effects (
Section

B.4
); and
C
, a superoperator-complexity measure (
Section

B.1
).
Suppose that there exists a zero-complexity operation
\mathcal{E}\neq\operatorname{id}
:
C(\mathcal{E})=0
.
Suppose, further, that
\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(P)\not\in\mathsf{P}
for some effect
P\in\mathsf{P}
.
Then
\mathsf{M}^{r=0}(\mathsf{P},C)\supsetneq\mathsf{P}
, since
\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(P)\in\mathsf{M}^{r=0}(\mathsf{P},C)
, by
(
87
)
.
If
\operatorname{id}
is the unique zero-complexity operation—or, more generally, if
\mathsf{P}
is closed under (the adjoint of) every zero-complexity operation—then
\mathsf{M}^{r=0}(\mathsf{P},C)=\mathsf{P}
.
In particular,
\operatorname{id}
is the unique zero-complexity operation whenever
C
is a circuit-complexity measure (
Section

B.1
).

B.5
POVM-effect-complexity sets from a POVM-effect-complexity measure

An alternative, equivalent formulation of a POVM-effect-complexity set (
Section

B.3
) arises from a complexity measure on individual POVM effects.
This formulation has the advantage of using a primitive formally similar to a superoperator-complexity measure (
Section

B.1
).
Consequently, some readers may find POVM-effect-complexity sets more intuitive in this formulation.

Definition 15

(POVM-effect-complexity measure)
.

Let
S
denote a composition of
N
quantum subsystems:
S=S_{1}S_{2}\ldots S_{N}
.
For every subset
I\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,N}\}
, let
S_{I}
denote the composition of the subsystems in
\{{S_{i}}\}_{i\in I}
.
A
POVM-effect-complexity measure
on
S
is a family of functions
F_{S_{I}}
that map POVM effects on
S_{I}
to
\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{{\infty}\}
, for all
I\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,N}\}
.
The functions have the following properties, for every
I\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,N}\}
:

1.

The identity effect has zero complexity:
F_{S_{I}}(\mathds{1}_{S_{I}})=0
.

2.

No parallel composition of POVM effects has a complexity greater than the sum of the individual effects’ complexities:

F_{S_{I}S_{J}}(Q_{1}\otimes Q_{2})\leqslant F_{S_{I}}(Q_{1})+F_{S_{J}}(Q_{2})
for all
J
disjoint from
I
.

For convenience, we denote a POVM-effect-complexity measure by its function
F_{S}
.
Every POVM-effect-complexity measure induces a family of POVM-effect-complexity sets (
Section

B.3
), as per the following definition.

Definition 16

(POVM-effect-complexity sets
from a POVM-effect-complexity measure)
.

Let
S=S_{1}S_{2}\ldots S_{N}
denote a quantum system; and
F_{S}
, a POVM-effect-complexity measure, as in
Section

B.5
.
A
family of POVM-effect-complexity sets
on
\mathscr{H}_{S}
, associated with
F_{S}
, is a family
\{{\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S_{I}}\left({F_{S_{I}}}\right)}\}_{r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
of sets of POVM effects.
Each
\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S_{I}}\left({F_{S_{I}}}\right)
denotes the set of POVM effects on
S_{I}
of complexities
\leqslant r
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S_{I}}\left({F_{S_{I}}}\right)\coloneqq\left\{{Q\>:\>F_{S_{I}}(Q)\leqslant r}\right\}\ .

(88)

One can straightforwardly check that the sets
\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S_{I}}\left({F_{S_{I}}}\right)
in
(
88
)
satisfy the conditions of
Section

B.3
.

We now construct a POVM-effect-complexity measure from simple POVM effects and a superoperator-complexity measure, providing an alternative way to define the sets
\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S_{I}}\left({\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}},C_{S_{I}}}\right)
in
(
87
)
.
Let
S
denote a quantum system; and
F_{S}
, a POVM-effect-complexity measure, as in
Section

B.5
.
Let
\mathsf{P}_{S}
denote a set of simple POVM effects (
Section

B.4
).
The following prescription, for all subsets
I\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,N}\}
, defines a POVM-effect-complexity measure:

\displaystyle F_{S_{I}}(Q)\coloneqq\inf\left\{{C_{S_{I}}(\mathcal{E})\,:\,P\in\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}},\,\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(P)=Q}\right\}\ .

(89)

Proposition 17
.

The sets
F_{S_{I}}
constructed in
(
89
)
define a POVM-effect-complexity measure.

Proof.

We verify that the sets
F_{S_{I}}
have both the properties in
Section

B.5
.
Property
1
holds because
C_{S_{I}}\left({\operatorname{id}_{S_{I}}}\right)=0
(
Section

B.1
),
\mathds{1}_{S_{I}}\in\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}}
(
Section

B.4
), and
\operatorname{id}_{S_{I}}^{\dagger}\left({\mathds{1}_{S_{I}}}\right)=\mathds{1}_{S_{I}}
.
To show that Property
2
holds, we consider any POVM effects
Q_{1}
on
S_{I}
and
Q_{2}
on
S_{J}
, with
I
and
J
disjoint.
Let
P_{1}\in\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}}
, and let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
denote an operation on
S_{I}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\dagger}(P_{1})=Q_{1}
.
Similarly, let
P_{2}\in\mathsf{P}_{S_{J}}
, and let
\mathcal{E}_{2}
denote an operation on
S_{J}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\dagger}(P_{2})=Q_{2}
.

\mathcal{E}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{2}
is a candidate for the
F_{S_{I}S_{J}}(Q_{1}\otimes Q_{2})
optimization in
(
89
)
, since
P_{1}\otimes P_{2}\in\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}S_{J}}
(
Section

B.4
) and
(\mathcal{E}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{2})^{\dagger}(P_{1}\otimes P_{2})=Q_{1}\otimes Q_{2}
.
Hence,
F_{S_{I}S_{J}}(Q_{1}\otimes Q_{2})\leqslant C_{S_{I}S_{J}}\left({\mathcal{E}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{2}}\right)
.
Furthermore,
C_{S_{I}S_{J}}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{2})\leqslant C_{S_{I}}\left({\mathcal{E}_{1}}\right)+C_{S_{J}}\left({\mathcal{E}_{2}}\right)
(
Section

B.1
), so
F_{S_{I}S_{J}}(Q_{1}\otimes Q_{2})\leqslant C_{S_{I}}\left({\mathcal{E}_{1}}\right)+C_{S_{J}}\left({\mathcal{E}_{2}}\right)
.
We obtain
F_{S_{I}S_{J}}(Q_{1}\otimes Q_{2})\leqslant F_{S_{I}}(Q_{1})+F_{S_{J}}(Q_{2})
by taking the infimum of the last inequality with respect to all candidates
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
for the
F_{S_{I}}(Q_{1})
and
F_{S_{J}}(Q_{2})
optimizations in
(
89
)
, respectively.
∎

A POVM-effect-complexity measure constructed as in
(
89
)
, with a circuit-superoperator-complexity measure (
Section

B.1
), is called a
circuit-POVM-effect-complexity measure
.
The POVM-effect-complexity measure in
(
89
)
induces, via
(
88
)
, the sets
\mathsf{M}^{r}_{S_{I}}\left({\mathsf{P}_{S_{I}},C_{S_{I}}}\right)
in
(
87
)
.

B.6
Examples of POVM-effect-complexity sets

Any set of simple POVM effects (
Section

B.4
) and any unitary-complexity measure (
Section

B.1
) determine a family of POVM-effect-complexity sets, as per
(
87
)
.
In this section, we provide natural examples of simple POVM effects and unitary-complexity measures on
n
qubits.

Examples of simple POVM effects

Among the POVM effects that one can directly implement in a laboratory, local effects are often the most feasible.
The following sets of simple POVM effects contain only tensor products.

•

The set of tensor products of the single-qubit projectors
\lvert{0}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{0}\rvert
and
\mathds{1}_{2}
.
We use this choice throughout the main text [see
(
10
)
], as is natural in the context of erasure.

•

The set of tensor products of single-qubit projectors.
This choice is natural if one can easily perform any single-qubit gate before implementing a projector onto a single-qubit state, e.g.,
\lvert{0}\rangle
.

•

The set of tensor-product POVM effects.
If performing nonlocal operations is hard, local operations are a natural choice for simple effects.
Tensor-product POVM effects generalize tensor-product projectors.

Importantly, there exist POVM effects that one cannot implement by first applying a unitary and then implementing a tensor-product POVM effect.
For instance, the two-qubit effect
\frac{1}{2}\left({\lvert{00}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{00}\rvert+\lvert{01}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{01}\rvert+\lvert{10}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{10}\rvert}\right)
is a rank-3 operator, unlike any tensor-product two-qubit effect: operator rank is invariant under unitary transformation, so the given effect is not unitarily equivalent to any tensor-product effect.

Examples of unitary-complexity measures

A natural unitary-complexity measure on
n
qubits is the circuit complexity associated with a two-qubit gate set
\mathcal{G}
(
Section

B.1
).
Depending on one’s experimental capabilities, one can consider circuits with all-to-all connectivity (any gate in
\mathcal{G}
can act on any two qubits); geometric locality (any gate in
\mathcal{G}
can act on only two neighboring qubits); or intermediate connectivity (some gates in
\mathcal{G}
can act on any two qubits, and other gates can act on only two neighboring qubits).
Convenient choices for
\mathcal{G}
include the following:

•

The set of two-qubit gates:
\mathcal{G}=\mathrm{SU}({4})
.
This choice is widespread in studies of random circuits
Brandão et al. 2016
;
Dalzell et al. 2022
.
For instance, this choice has elucidated the linear growth, with time, of exact circuit complexity under random circuits
Haferkamp_21_Linear
;
Li_22_ShortProofsOfLinearGrowth
.

•

The following Clifford gates: Hadamard,
S
, and
CNOT
gates.
This set generates quantum circuits that can be efficiently simulated classically
Gottesman 1998
.
The set is not universal, and almost all unitaries have infinite complexities with respect to the set.

•

Any finite, universal set of two-qubit gates, such as the Clifford +
T
gate set.
This set can approximate every unitary arbitrarily well with a finite-complexity unitary
NielsenC10
.
In this setting, the circuit complexity grows algebraically under random quantum circuits with gates drawn from a finite gate set
Brandão et al. 2021
;
brandao2016local
;
haferkamp2022random
.

Our definition of unitary complexity accommodates other notions of complexity, such as the Nielsen complexity
Nielsen 2005
;
Nielsen_06_Quantum
;
Nielsen et al. 2006b
;
Dowling and Nielsen 2008
.
Another example is the minimum
T
-count of any Clifford +
T
circuit that approximates a given unitary.
(In the latter example, different gates contribute different amounts of complexity.
Each
T
gate contributes one unit of complexity; and each Clifford gate, zero units.

Appendix C
Hypothesis-testing (relative) entropy

In a hypothesis test, one receives either
\rho
or
\sigma
, and guesses which state one received.
In the most general strategy to distinguish
\rho
and
\sigma
, one performs a two-outcome measurement
\{Q,\mathds{1}-Q\}
.
One guesses
\rho
if
Q
obtains and guesses
\sigma
otherwise.
Suppose that one must, if the state is
\rho
, guess
\rho
with a probability
\geqslant\eta\in(0,1]
.
The minimum probability of wrongly guessing
\rho
defines the
hypothesis-testing relative entropy
.

Definition 18

(Hypothesis-testing relative entropy)
.

Let
\rho
denote any subnormalized state, and
\Gamma
any positive-semidefinite operator, that act on the same Hilbert space.
Let
\eta\in(0,\tr(\rho)]
.
The hypothesis-testing relative entropy is defined as
Datta_2013_HypothesisTesting
;
Wang and Renner 2012
;
Dupuis et al. 2013

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)\coloneqq-\log\;\left({\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leqslant Q\leqslant\mathds{1}\\
\tr({Q\rho})\geqslant\eta\end{subarray}}\left\{{\frac{\tr\left({Q\Gamma}\right)}{\eta}}\right\}}\right)\ .

(90)

In a hypothesis test, the least probability of erroneously rejecting
\sigma
, using a strategy that successfully accepts
\rho
with a probability
\geqslant\eta
, is
\eta\exp\bm{(}-D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\sigma)\bm{)}
.
Every optimal POVM effect
Q
satisfies
\tr({Q\rho})=\eta
.
If any such
Q
violated this equation, one could achieve a better objective value with
Q^{\prime}\coloneqq\eta Q/\tr({Q\rho})\leqslant Q
.
Consequently, if
\Gamma=\rho
, then
D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)=0
. In this case, every
Q
satisfying
\tr({Q\rho})=\eta
is optimal.

There always exists an optimal effect for the
D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}
optimization, since the domain of optimization is compact.
This fact justifies the use of a minimum, instead of an infimum, in
(
90
)
.

Definition 19

(Hypothesis-testing entropy)
.

Let
\rho
denote any subnormalized state.
Let
\eta\in(0,\tr(\rho)]
.
The hypothesis-testing entropy is defined as
Dupuis et al. 2013

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)\coloneqq-D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\mathds{1})=\log\;\left({\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leqslant Q\leqslant\mathds{1}\\
\tr({Q\rho})\geqslant\eta\end{subarray}}\left\{{\frac{\tr\left({Q}\right)}{\eta}}\right\}}\right)\ .

(91)

The hypothesis-testing entropy and relative entropy are related to smooth entropies, including the min- and max-relative entropies
Datta 2009
;
Dupuis et al. 2013
.
The smooth entropies quantify the optimal efficiencies of operational tasks performed in the absence of complexity restrictions.
Such tasks feature finitely many copies of a quantum state or random variable, as well as finite failure probabilities.

One can express the hypothesis-testing relative entropy
(
90
)
as a semidefinite program.
The dual problem takes the form
Dupuis et al. 2013

D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)=-\log\left({\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}X\geqslant 0,\,\mu\geqslant 0\\
\mu\rho\leqslant\Gamma+X\end{subarray}}\left\{{\mu-\frac{\tr\left({X}\right)}{\eta}}\right\}}\right)\ .\\

(92)

We now reformulate the hypothesis-testing relative entropy.
