equals the set of gates on
n
qubits, in
G
, with arbitrary (fixed) connectivity.

Let
\mathtt{E}=\{{p_{j},U_{j}}\}
denote a random ensemble of unitary operators.
Each unitary
U_{j}\in\mathrm{U}(2^{n})
is chosen with the probability
p_{j}
.
For each integer
k>0
, we define the
k
-twirling superoperator
associated with
\mathtt{E}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathtt{E}}^{(k)}(\cdot)\coloneqq\sum_{j}p_{j}\,U_{j}^{\otimes k}\,(\cdot)\,U_{j}^{\dagger\,\otimes k}\ .

(184)

The
k
-twirling superoperator
associated with the Haar measure is

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Haar}}^{(k)}(\cdot)\coloneqq\int dU\,U^{\otimes k}\,(\cdot)\,U^{\dagger\,\otimes k}\ .

(185)

dU
denotes the Haar measure on the unitary group
\mathrm{U}(2^{n})
.
If
\mathcal{M}_{\mathtt{E}}^{(k)}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Haar}}^{(k)}
are sufficiently close in diamond distance,
\mathtt{E}
is an approximate
k
-design.

Definition 51

(Approximate unitary
k
-design)
.

Let
\epsilon\geqslant 0
, and let
k>0
denote an integer.
An ensemble
\mathtt{E}=\{{p_{j},U_{j}}\}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate unitary
k
-design
if

\displaystyle\big\lVert{\mathcal{M}_{\mathtt{E}}^{(k)}-\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Haar}}^{(k)}}\big\rVert_{\diamond}\leqslant\frac{\epsilon}{2^{nk}}\ .

(186)

We employ the definition of an approximate
k
-design used in Ref.
Haferkamp 2022
; this definition differs from that used in Ref.
Brandão et al. 2021
.
In Ref.
Brandão et al. 2021
,
\left({k!/2^{2nk}}\right)\,\epsilon
replaces the upper bound in
(
186
)
.
Therefore, an
\epsilon
-approximate
k
-design, according to
Appendix

F
, is a
\left({2^{nk}\epsilon/k!}\right)
-approximate
k
-design, according to Ref.
Brandão et al. 2021
.

Following Ref.
Brandão et al. 2021
, we rely on the fact that random circuits generate approximate
k
-designs
Brandão et al. 2016
;
Haferkamp 2022
;
Haferkamp and Hunter-Jones 2021
;
Chen et al. 2024
.
In particular, we utilize the following result, which shows that certain random circuits of a depth
t>0
are approximate
k
-designs, with
k\sim t
.

Theorem 52

(
Chen et al. 2024
)
.

Consider an
n
-qubit system.
Let
\tilde{\epsilon}>0
and
c\geqslant 0
be independent of
n
.
Let
V
denote a circuit of a depth
t>0
, with staggered layers of nearest-neighbor two-qubit gates (the “brickwork” layout).
Suppose each of the circuit’s gates is chosen at random from the Haar measure on
\mathrm{SU}({4})
.
Then
V
is a
\big({2^{-cnk}\tilde{\epsilon}}\big)
-approximate
k
-design, with

\displaystyle k=\min\left\{{2^{n/2-O\left({\sqrt{n}}\right)}\ ,\ \frac{t}{\poly(n)}}\right\}\ .

(187)

The dependence of
k
on
\tilde{\epsilon}
is hidden in the coefficient implied by the
\poly(n)
notation.

We briefly show how condition
(
187
)
arises from the results in Ref.
Chen et al. 2024
.
Theorem 1.5 in Ref.
Haferkamp 2022
implies the following statement.
Let
0<k\leqslant 2^{n/2-\sqrt{n}-2}
and let
\epsilon>0
.
Then there exists
C>0
such that depth-
t
brickwork random circuits form an
\epsilon
-approximate
k
-design whenever

\displaystyle t\geqslant Cn^{3}[2nk+\log_{2}(1/\epsilon)]\eqqcolon t_{0}(k)\ .

(188)

Setting
\epsilon=2^{-cnk}\tilde{\epsilon}
, with
\tilde{\epsilon}\in(0,1)
a constant of
n
, and using
k\leqslant 2^{n/2}
, we find that

\displaystyle t_{0}(k)\leqslant\poly(n)\,k\ .

(189)

Consider now the setting of
Appendix

F
.
The
k
specified in
(
187
)
implies
t\geqslant t_{0}(k)
, ensuring that Corollary 1 in Ref.
Haferkamp 2022
applies.

The bound
(
47
)
—and hence
Section

V.4
—follows from
Appendix

F
and the following proposition.

Proposition 53

(Complexity-entropy lower bound with an approximate
k
-design (simple))
.

Let
\lvert{\psi_{0}}\rangle
denote any pure
n
-qubit state.
Let
\tilde{\epsilon}\in(0,1)
.
Assume that
k
is even.
Let
V
denote a circuit sampled from a
\big({2^{-nk}\tilde{\epsilon}}\big)
-approximate
k
-design.
Let
r\geqslant 0
and
\eta\in(0,1]
.
Suppose that

\displaystyle r\leqslant\frac{k\left[{\frac{n}{2}-\log_{2}(k)-\log_{2}(4/\eta)}\right]-\left\{{3n+\log_{2}(n)\left[{1+\log_{2}(4/\eta)}\right]}\right\}}{c_{1}\left[{2n+\log_{2}({2/\eta})}\right]^{4}+\log_{2}({n+1})+1}\ ,

(190)

wherein
c_{1}>0
denotes a constant independent of
n
.
Then

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)\geqslant H_{\mathrm{h}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)\geqslant n\log(2)-\log\left({\frac{4}{\eta}}\right)\ ,

(191)

except with a probability
\leqslant e^{-\Omega(n)}
over the sampling of
V
.
Here,
H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}
is defined with respect to
\mathsf{M}^{r}_{\mathrm{SU}({4})}
.

We specify the constant
c_{1}
in the proof of
Appendix

F
.
To prove the proposition, we first use the Solovay-Kitaev theorem
Dawson and Nielsen 2005
;
BookNielsenChuang2000
;
Kitaev et al. 2002
;
Oszmaniec et al. 2021

to approximate
\mathrm{SU}({4})
with a finite, universal gate set.
We then apply the following lemma, whose proof closely follows that of Theorem 8 of Ref.
Brandão et al. 2021
.

Lemma 54

(Gate-set complexity-entropy lower bound for approximate
k
-designs)
.

Let
\lvert{\psi_{0}}\rangle
denote any pure
n
-qubit state.
Let
\tilde{\epsilon}\in(0,1)
.
Assume that
k
is even.
Let
V
denote a circuit sampled from a
\big({2^{-nk}\tilde{\epsilon}}\big)
-approximate
k
-design.
Let
r\geqslant 0
,
\bar{\eta}\in(0,1]
,
c\in(0,1)
, and
g>0
.
Suppose that

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\log_{2}\left({[n+1]\lvert{G}\rvert}\right)}\left({k\bigg[{\frac{n}{2}-\log_{2}(k)-\log_{2}\left({\frac{1}{\bar{\eta}\,(1-c)}}\right)}\bigg]-\left\{{(g+1)n+2+\log_{2}(n)\left[1+\log_{2}\left({\frac{1}{c\bar{\eta}}}\right)\right]}\right\}}\right)\ .

(192)

Then

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{h},\,G}^{{r},\,{\bar{\eta}}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant n\log(2)-\log\left({\frac{1}{c\bar{\eta}}}\right)\ ,

(193)

except with a probability
\leqslant 2^{-gn}
over the sampling of
V
.
Here, the reduced complexity entropy
H_{\mathrm{h},\,G}^{{r},\,{\bar{\eta}}}
(
Section

D.5.1
) is defined with respect to
\mathsf{M}^{r}_{G}
, wherein
G
is finite.

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle m\coloneqq\biggl\lfloor n-\log_{2}\left({\frac{1}{c\bar{\eta}}}\right)\biggr\rfloor=n-\biggl\lceil\log_{2}\left({\frac{1}{c\bar{\eta}}}\right)\biggr\rceil\ .

(194)

(
193
)
holds, except with the probability

\displaystyle p_{<}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Pr_{V}\left[{H_{\mathrm{h},\,G}^{{r},\,{\bar{\eta}}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)<n\log(2)-\log\left({\frac{1}{c\bar{\eta}}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Pr_{V}\left[{H_{\mathrm{h},\,G}^{{r},\,{\bar{\eta}}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)\leqslant n\log(2)-\log\left({\frac{1}{c\bar{\eta}}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{V}\left[{H_{\mathrm{h},\,G}^{{r},\,{\bar{\eta}}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)\leqslant m\log(2)}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Pr_{V}\left[{\eta_{\mathrm{h},\,G}^{{r},\,{m\log(2)}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)\geqslant\bar{\eta}}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{V}\left[{\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathsf{M}^{r}\\
\log_{2}\bm{(}\tr({Q})\bm{)}=m\end{subarray}}\left\{{\tr\left({QV\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}}\right)}\right\}\geqslant\bar{\eta}}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathsf{M}^{r}\\
\log_{2}\bm{(}\tr({Q})\bm{)}=m\end{subarray}}\Pr_{V}\left[{\tr\left({QV\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}}\right)\geqslant\bar{\eta}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathsf{M}^{r}\\
\log_{2}\bm{(}\tr({Q})\bm{)}=m\end{subarray}}\Pr_{V}\left[{\tr\left({\left[{Q-2^{m-n}\mathds{1}}\right]V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}}\right)\geqslant\bar{\eta}-2^{m-n}}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathsf{M}^{r}\\
\log_{2}\bm{(}\tr({Q})\bm{)}=m\end{subarray}}\Pr_{V}\left[{\left\lvert{\tr\left({\left[{Q-2^{m-n}\mathds{1}}\right]V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}}\right)}\right\rvert\geqslant\left\lvert{(1-c)\bar{\eta}}\right\rvert}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathsf{M}^{r}\\
\log_{2}\bm{(}\tr({Q})\bm{)}=m\end{subarray}}\Pr_{V}\left[{\left\{{\tr\left({\left[{Q-2^{m-n}\mathds{1}}\right]V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}}\right)}\right\}^{k}\geqslant\left\{{(1-c)\bar{\eta}}\right\}^{k}}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathsf{M}^{r}\\
\log_{2}\bm{(}\tr({Q})\bm{)}=m\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{V}\left[{\left\{{\tr\left({[Q-2^{m-n}\mathds{1}]V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}}\right)}\right\}^{k}}\right]}{\left[{(1-c)\bar{\eta}}\right]^{k}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathsf{M}^{r}\\
\log_{2}\bm{(}\tr({Q})\bm{)}=m\end{subarray}}2\left({\frac{k}{2^{n/2}(1-c)\bar{\eta}}}\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=2\left({\frac{k}{2^{n/2}(1-c)\bar{\eta}}}\right)^{k}\left\lvert{\left\{{Q\in\mathsf{M}^{r}\,:\;\log_{2}\bm{(}\tr({Q})\bm{)}=m}\right\}}\right\rvert\ .

(195)

The second equality follows because, by the form of
\mathsf{M}^{r}
,
H_{\mathrm{h}}^{{r},\,{\bar{\eta}}}\left(V\,\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)
is an integer multiple of
\log(2)
.
The second inequality follows from
Section

D.5.2
:
H_{\mathrm{h}}^{{r},\,{\bar{\eta}}}\left(V\,\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)\leqslant m\log(2)
implies that
\eta_{\mathrm{h}}^{{r},\,{m\log(2)}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)\geqslant\bar{\eta}
.
The third equality follows from
(
162
)
; here, the supremum is a maximum because
G
—and hence
\mathsf{M}^{r}_{G}
—is finite.
The third inequality is
a crude union bound.
The fourth equality introduces the traceless part of each
Q
:
Q-2^{m-n}\mathds{1}=Q-\tr({Q})\cdot 2^{-n}\mathds{1}
.
The fourth inequality follows because

\left\lvert{\tr\left({\left[{Q-2^{m-n}\mathds{1}}\right]V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}}\right)}\right\rvert\geqslant\tr\left({\left[{Q-2^{m-n}\mathds{1}}\right]V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}}\right)

(196)

and because
\bar{\eta}-2^{m-n}\geqslant(1-c)\bar{\eta}>0
, since
2^{m-n}=2^{-\lceil\log_{2}\left({1/c\bar{\eta}}\right)\rceil}\leqslant 2^{-\log_{2}\left({1/c\bar{\eta}}\right)}=c\bar{\eta}
.
The fifth equality follows because, by assumption,
k
is even.
The fifth inequality follows from Markov’s inequality: for any non-negative random variable
X
and any
\tau>0
,
\Pr[X\geqslant\tau]=\mathbb{E}[X]/\tau
.
The last inequality follows from Corollary 24 of Ref.
Brandão et al. 2021
:
\mathbb{E}_{V}\left[{\left\{{\tr([Q-2^{m-n}\mathds{1}]V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger})}\right\}^{k}}\right]\leqslant\left({1+\epsilon}\right)\left({k/2^{n/2}}\right)^{k}\leqslant 2\left({k/2^{n/2}}\right)^{k}
, wherein
\epsilon=\frac{2^{nk}}{k!}2^{-nk}\tilde{\epsilon}\leqslant 1
(recall the alternative convention used for
\epsilon
-approximate designs in Ref.
Brandão et al. 2021
).

By the form of
\mathsf{M}^{r}
,
\left\lvert{\left\{{Q\in\mathsf{M}^{r}\,:\;\log_{2}\bm{(}\tr({Q})\bm{)}=m}\right\}}\right\rvert\leqslant N_{r}\binom{n}{m}
.
The
N_{r}
denotes the number of circuits that one can compose from
\leqslant r
gates in
\mathcal{G}
.

\binom{n}{m}
is the number of projectors that, up to a permutation of qubits, equal
\mathds{1}_{2}^{\otimes m}\otimes\lvert{0^{n-m}}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{0^{n-m}}\rvert
.
By Eq. (B21) of Ref.
Brandão et al. 2021
,
N_{r}\leqslant 2^{n+1}(n+1)^{r}\lvert{G}\rvert^{r}
.
Moreover,

\binom{n}{m}=\binom{n}{n-m}\leqslant n^{n-m}=n^{\lceil\log_{2}\left({1/c\bar{\eta}}\right)\rceil}\leqslant n^{1+\log_{2}\left({1/c\bar{\eta}}\right)}
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\left\{{Q\in\mathsf{M}^{r}\,:\;\log_{2}\bm{(}\tr({Q})\bm{)}=m}\right\}}\right\rvert\leqslant 2^{n+1}(n+1)^{r}\lvert{G}\rvert^{r}\cdot n^{1+\log_{2}\left({1/c\bar{\eta}}\right)}\ ,

(197)

so
p_{<}\leqslant 2^{a+rb-ky}
,
where

\displaystyle a\coloneqq n+2+\log_{2}(n)\left[{1+\log_{2}\left({\frac{1}{c\bar{\eta}}}\right)}\right]\ ,\;\;\;b\coloneqq\log_{2}\left({[n+1]\lvert{G}\rvert}\right)\ ,\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;y\coloneqq\frac{n}{2}-\log_{2}(k)-\log_{2}\left({\frac{1}{(1-c)\bar{\eta}}}\right)\ .

(198)

By
(
192
)
,
r\leqslant\frac{1}{b}\left[{ky-(gn+a)}\right]
, so
a+rb-ky\leqslant-gn
.
Thus,
p_{<}\leqslant 2^{-gn}
.
∎

Proof of
Appendix F
.

Let
\epsilon^{\prime}\coloneqq\eta/2
.
Let
G\subset\mathrm{SU}({4})
denote any finite gate set that forms an
(\epsilon^{\prime}/4^{n})
-net on
\mathrm{SU}({4})
.
One can build such a gate set using the Solovay-Kitaev theorem
Dawson and Nielsen 2005
;
BookNielsenChuang2000
;
Kitaev et al. 2002
;
Oszmaniec et al. 2021

as follows.
Consider any finite, universal gate set
G_{0}\subset\mathrm{SU}({4})
, such as the Clifford +
T
gate set (see
Section

B.6
).
By the Solovay-Kitaev theorem, one can approximate any unitary
U\in\mathrm{SU}({4})
to the error
\epsilon^{\prime}
with at most
N\coloneqq c_{G_{0}}\left[{\log_{2}(4^{n}/\epsilon^{\prime})}\right]^{4}=c_{G_{0}}\left[{2n+\log_{2}({2/\eta})}\right]^{4}
gates in
G_{0}
.
The
c_{G_{0}}>0
is a universal constant depending only on
G_{0}
.
Now, let
G
consist of all the circuits that one can compose from
\leqslant N
gates in
G_{0}
.
One can approximate any unitary
U\in\mathrm{SU}({4})
to the error
\epsilon^{\prime}
by some circuit in
G
.
The number of circuits that one can compose from exactly
t>0
gates in
G_{0}
is
\leqslant\lvert{G_{0}}\rvert^{t}
, so

\displaystyle\lvert{G}\rvert\leqslant\sum_{t=1}^{N}\lvert{G_{0}}\rvert^{t}=\frac{\lvert{G_{0}}\rvert}{\lvert{G_{0}}\rvert-1}\left({\lvert{G_{0}}\rvert^{N}-1}\right)\leqslant 2\lvert{G_{0}}\rvert^{N}\ .

(199)

Hence,

\displaystyle\log_{2}\left({\lvert{G}\rvert}\right)\leqslant N\log_{2}\left({\left\lvert{G_{0}}\right\rvert}\right)+1=c_{1}\left[{2n+\log_{2}\left({\frac{2}{\eta}}\right)}\right]^{4}+1\ ,

(200)

wherein
c_{1}\coloneqq c_{G_{0}}\,\log_{2}\left({\left\lvert{G_{0}}\right\rvert}\right)
.

We now apply
Appendix

F
.
Set

\displaystyle g=1\ ,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;c=\frac{1}{2}\ ,\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

\displaystyle\text{and}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\bar{\eta}=\eta-\epsilon^{\prime}=\frac{\eta}{2}\ .

(201)

r
satisfies condition
(
192
)
in the lemma, since the right-hand side of
(
190
)
lower-bounds the right-hand side of
(
192
)
, as one can verify using the following inequalities:

\displaystyle\log_{2}\left({[n+1]\left\lvert{G}\right\rvert}\right)\leqslant c_{1}\left[{2n+\log_{2}\left({\frac{2}{\eta}}\right)}\right]^{4}+\log_{2}({n+1})+1\ ,

(202)

\displaystyle\log_{2}\left({\frac{1}{(1-c)\bar{\eta}}}\right)=\log_{2}\left({\frac{4}{\eta}}\right)\ ,

(203)

\displaystyle\text{and}\;\;\;\;\;2\leqslant n\quad\Rightarrow\quad(g+1)n+2+\log_{2}\left({\frac{1}{c\bar{\eta}}}\right)\leqslant 3n+\log_{2}\left({\frac{4}{\eta}}\right)\ .

(204)

Thus, by
Appendix

F
,

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{h},\,G}^{{r},\,{\eta-\epsilon^{\prime}}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)=H_{\mathrm{h},\,G}^{{r},\,{\bar{\eta}}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)\geqslant n\log(2)-\log\left({\frac{1}{c\bar{\eta}}}\right)=n\log(2)-\log\left({\frac{4}{\eta}}\right)\ ,

(205)

except with a probability
\leqslant 2^{-n}
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)\geqslant H_{\mathrm{h}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)\geqslant H_{\mathrm{h},\,G}^{{r},\,{\eta}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)\geqslant H_{\mathrm{h},\,G}^{{r},\,{\eta-\epsilon^{\prime}}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)\ ,

(206)

wherein
H_{\mathrm{h}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}
is defined with respect to
\mathsf{M}^{r}_{\mathrm{SU}({4})}
.
The first inequality follows from
Section

D.5.1
.
The second inequality follows because
\mathsf{M}^{r}_{G}\subset\mathsf{M}^{r}_{\mathrm{SU}({4})}
: every candidate
Q\in\mathsf{M}^{r}_{G}
for the
H_{\mathrm{h},\,G}^{{r},\,{\eta}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)
optimization belongs to
\mathsf{M}^{r}_{\mathrm{SU}({4})}
and is therefore a candidate for the
H_{\mathrm{h}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)
optimization.
The third inequality follows from
Section

D.2
(via
Section

D.5.1
).
Therefore,

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)\geqslant H_{\mathrm{h}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}\left(V\psi_{0}V^{\dagger}\right)\geqslant n\log(2)-\log\left({\frac{4}{\eta}}\right)\ ,

(207)

except with a probability at most exponentially small in
n
.
∎

Appendix G
Entanglement bounds on the complexity entropy

Here, we use entanglement measures to bound the complexity entropy, proving the results in
Section

V.3
.
We restrict our attention to a spatially one-dimensional chain
S
of
n\geqslant 2
qubits:
S=S_{1}S_{2}\ldots S_{n}
.
Let
\left\{{\mathsf{M}^{r}}\right\}
denote the family of POVM-effect-complexity sets (
Section

B.3
) defined by
(
11
)
, wherein the computational gate set
\mathcal{G}
contains only unitary operations that can act nontrivially only on two neighboring qubits in
S
.
Furthermore, we define the
potential entangling power
of
\mathcal{G}
(cf. Ref.
Eisert 2021
) as

\displaystyle e(\mathcal{G})\coloneqq\sup_{U\in\mathcal{G}}\left\{{e(U)}\right\}\ ,\quad\text{wherein}\quad e(U)\coloneqq\min\left\{{\lVert{H}\rVert\,:\;H=H^{\dagger},\;\exists\chi\in\mathbb{R}\text{ such that }e^{-iH}=e^{-i\chi}U}\right\}\ .

(208)

In general,
e(\mathcal{G})
can be as large as
\pi
.
However, our entanglement bounds are most interesting when
\mathcal{G}
contains only operations that act weakly and remain near the identity operator (up to an overall phase).
In this case,
e(\mathcal{G})\approx e(\mathds{1})=0
.

In the following, we use the
quantum mutual information
.
Let
A
and
B
denote distinct quantum systems, and let
\sigma_{AB}
denote any state of
AB
.
The quantum mutual information of
\sigma_{AB}
is defined in terms of the von Neumann entropy as

\displaystyle I(A\,:\,B)_{\sigma}\coloneqq H(A)_{\sigma}+H(B)_{\sigma}-H(AB)_{\sigma}=H(A)_{\sigma}-H(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}B)_{\sigma}\ .

(209)

For any state
\rho
of the qubit chain
S
, we define the entanglement measure

\displaystyle E(\rho)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}I(S_{1}\ldots S_{j}\,:\,S_{j+1}\ldots S_{n})_{\rho}\ .

(210)

E(\rho)
quantifies the average amount of correlation, as quantified by the mutual information, between any bipartition of
S
into subsystems of neighboring qubits.
We now prove the bounds in
Section

V.3
and in
(
45
)
.

Theorem 55

(Continuity bound on
E(\rho)
under one operation)
.

Let
\rho
denote any quantum state of
S
.
Let
U
denote any unitary that can act nontrivially only on two neighboring qubits in
S
.
It holds that

\displaystyle\left\lvert{E\left({U\rho U^{\dagger}}\right)-E\left({\rho}\right)}\right\rvert\leqslant\frac{1}{n-1}\min\left\{{8\log(2)\ ,\ 8\nu\log(2)+3({1+\nu})\,h\left({\frac{\nu}{1+\nu}}\right)}\right\}\ .

(211)

The
h(x)\coloneqq-x\log(x)-({1-x})\log({1-x})
denotes the binary entropy function, and

\displaystyle\nu\coloneqq\sin\left({\min\left\{{e(U),\frac{\pi}{2}}\right\}}\right)\ .

(212)

Proof.

Let
\rho^{\prime}
denote the state that results from evolving
\rho
under
U
:
\rho^{\prime}\coloneqq U\rho U^{\dagger}
.
For all
j\in\{{1,2,\ldots,n-1}\}
, we write, for short,
A_{j}\coloneqq S_{1}S_{2}\ldots S_{j}
and
B_{j+1}\coloneqq S_{j+1}S_{j+2}\ldots S_{n}
.
By
(
210
)
,

\displaystyle E(\rho^{\prime})-E(\rho)=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\left[{I(A_{j}\,:\,B_{j+1})_{\rho^{\prime}}-I(A_{j}\,:\,B_{j+1})_{\rho}}\right]\ .

(213)

Let
k,k+1\in\{{1,2,\ldots,n}\}
denote two qubits on which
U
can act nontrivially.
For all
j\neq k
,
I(A_{j}\,:\,B_{j+1})_{\rho^{\prime}}=I(A_{j}\,:\,B_{j+1})_{\rho}
:

U
can act nontrivially on qubits only in
A_{j}
or only in
B_{j+1}
, so

\displaystyle I(A_{j}\,:\,B_{j+1})_{\rho}

\displaystyle=H(A_{j})_{\rho}+H(B_{j+1})_{\rho}-H(A_{j}B_{j+1})_{\rho}

\displaystyle=H(A_{j})_{\rho^{\prime}}+H(B_{j+1})_{\rho^{\prime}}-H(A_{j}B_{j+1})_{\rho^{\prime}}=I(A_{j}\,:\,B_{j+1})_{\rho^{\prime}}\ .

(214)

The first and last equalities follow from
(
209
)
.
The middle equality follows because the von Neumann entropy is unitarily invariant.
Thus,

\displaystyle E(\rho^{\prime})-E(\rho)=\frac{1}{n-1}\left[{I(A_{k}\,:\,B_{k+1})_{\rho^{\prime}}-I(A_{k}\,:\,B_{k+1})_{\rho}}\right]\ .

(215)

Using the chain rule for the von Neumann entropy, we simultaneously re-express
I(A_{k}\,:\,B_{k+1})_{\rho}
and
I(A_{k}\,:\,B_{k+1})_{\rho^{\prime}}
:

\displaystyle I(A_{k}\,:\,B_{k+1})

\displaystyle=H(A_{k})+H(B_{k+1})-H(A_{k}B_{k+1})

\displaystyle=H(A_{k-1})+H(S_{k}\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}A_{k-1})+H(B_{k+2})+H(S_{k+1}\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}B_{k+2})-H(A_{k-1}B_{k+2})-H(S_{k}S_{k+1}\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}A_{k-1}B_{k+2})

\displaystyle=\underbrace{H(S_{k}\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}A_{k-1})+H(S_{k+1}\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}B_{k+2})-H(S_{k}S_{k+1}\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}A_{k-1}B_{k+2})}_{\textup{(I)}}+\underbrace{H(A_{k-1})+H(B_{k+2})-H(A_{k-1}B_{k+2})}_{\textup{(II)}}\ .

(216)

A_{k-1}\left({B_{k+2}}\right)
is trivial if
k=1
(
k=n-1
).
Since
U
can act nontrivially only on
S_{k}S_{k+1}
, the reduced states of
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
on
A_{k-1}B_{k+2}
coincide.
Hence, the terms in (II) have the same value whether evaluated on
\rho
or on
\rho^{\prime}
, so
(
215
)
equals the difference between the terms in (I) evaluated on
\rho
and on
\rho^{\prime}
.
We now upper-bound the absolute value of this difference.
Let
\mathcal{U}(\cdot)\coloneqq U({\cdot})U^{\dagger}
.
By the diamond norm’s definition
(
62
)
and by
Appendix

A
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\lVert{\rho^{\prime}-\rho}\rVert_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\lVert{\left({\mathcal{U}-\operatorname{id}}\right)\rho}\rVert_{1}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\lVert{\mathcal{U}-\operatorname{id}}\rVert_{\diamond}=\sin\left({\min\left\{{e(U),\frac{\pi}{2}}\right\}}\right)=\nu\ .

(217)

By the Alicki-Fannes-Winter continuity bound on the conditional entropy
Alicki and Fannes 2004
;
Winter 2016
(see Lemma 2 of Ref.
Winter 2016
),
(
217
)
implies that the terms in (I) obey the following bounds:

\displaystyle\left\lvert{H(S_{k}\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}A_{k-1})_{\rho^{\prime}}-H(S_{k}\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}A_{k-1})_{\rho}}\right\rvert\leqslant 2\nu\log\big({d_{S_{k}}}\big)+({1+\nu})\,h\left({\frac{\nu}{1+\nu}}\right)\ ,

(218a)

\displaystyle\left\lvert{H(S_{k+1}\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}B_{k+2})_{\rho^{\prime}}-H(S_{k+1}\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}B_{k+2})_{\rho}}\right\rvert\leqslant 2\nu\log\big({d_{S_{k+1}}}\big)+({1+\nu})\,h\left({\frac{\nu}{1+\nu}}\right)\ ,

(218b)

\displaystyle\text{and}\;\;\;\left\lvert{H(S_{k}S_{k+1}\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}A_{k-1}B_{k+2})_{\rho^{\prime}}-H(S_{k}S_{k+1}\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}A_{k-1}B_{k+2})_{\rho}}\right\rvert\leqslant 2\nu\log\big({d_{S_{k}}d_{S_{k+1}}}\big)+({1+\nu})\,h\left({\frac{\nu}{1+\nu}}\right)\ .

(218c)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\lvert{I(A_{k}\,:\,B_{k+1})_{\rho^{\prime}}-I(A_{k}\,:\,B_{k+1})_{\rho}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\nu\log\big({d_{S_{k}}^{2}d_{S_{k+1}}^{2}}\big)+3({1+\nu})\,h\left({\frac{\nu}{1+\nu}}\right)

\displaystyle=8\nu\log(2)+3({1+\nu})\,h\left({\frac{\nu}{1+\nu}}\right)\ .

(219)

The equality follows because
S_{k}
and
S_{k+1}
are qubits:
d_{S_{k}}=d_{S_{k+1}}=2
.
Moreover, each term in
(I)
can vary by at most the range of the term’s corresponding conditional entropy, so

\displaystyle\left\lvert{I(A_{k}\,:\,B_{k+1})_{\rho^{\prime}}-I(A_{k}\,:\,B_{k+1})_{\rho}}\right\rvert\leqslant 2\log\big({d_{S_{k}}}\big)+2\log\big({d_{S_{k+1}}}\big)+2\log\big({d_{S_{k}}d_{S_{k+1}}}\big)=2\log\big({d_{S_{k}}^{2}d_{S_{k+1}}^{2}}\big)=8\log(2)\ .

(220)

Combining
(
215
)
,
(
219
)
, and
(
220
)
yields

\displaystyle\left\lvert{E(\rho^{\prime})-E(\rho)}\right\rvert\leqslant\frac{1}{n-1}\min\left\{{8\log(2)\ ,\ 8\nu\log(2)+3({1+\nu})\,h\left({\frac{\nu}{1+\nu}}\right)}\right\}\ .

(221)

∎

It is convenient to define the worst-case upper bound in
(
211
)
among all the gates in
\mathcal{G}
.
Namely, let

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{G})\coloneqq\frac{1}{n-1}\min\left\{{8\log(2)\ ,\ 8\nu_{\mathcal{G}}\log(2)+3({1+\nu_{\mathcal{G}}})\,h\left({\frac{\nu_{\mathcal{G}}}{1+\nu_{\mathcal{G}}}}\right)}\right\}\ ,\;\;\text{where}\;\;\nu_{\mathcal{G}}\coloneqq\sin\left({\min\left\{{e(\mathcal{G}),\frac{\pi}{2}}\right\}}\right)\ .

(222)

By definition,
\mu(\mathcal{G})
always satisfies
\mu(\mathcal{G})\leqslant 8\log(2)/(n-1)
.

Theorem 56

(Entanglement bound on the complexity entropy)
.

Let
\rho
denote any quantum state of
S
.
Let
r\geqslant 0
and
\eta\in(0,1]
.
It holds that

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)\geqslant\frac{1}{\eta}\left[{E(\rho)-r\,\mu(\mathcal{G})+H(\rho)-2h(\eta)-(1-\eta)\,n\log(2)}\right]-2\log(\eta)\ .

(223)

The
h(x)\coloneqq-x\log(x)-({1-x})\log({1-x})
denotes the binary entropy function, and
H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}
is defined with respect to
\mathsf{M}^{r}
.

Proof.

Consider any
\zeta>0
.
There exists a
Q\in\mathsf{M}^{r}
such that
\tr({Q\rho})\geqslant\eta
and
\log\bm{(}\tr({Q})/\tr({Q\rho})\bm{)}\leqslant H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)+\zeta
.

Q=U^{\dagger}PU
for some effect
P\in\mathsf{M}^{r=0}
and for some unitary
U
satisfying
C_{\mathcal{G}}(U_{0})\leqslant r
.
Let
\rho^{\prime}\coloneqq U\rho U^{\dagger}
.

\tr({P\rho^{\prime}})=\tr({Q\rho})\geqslant\eta
, so
P
is a candidate for the
H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r=0},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho^{\prime}-NoValue-)
optimization:

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r=0},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho^{\prime}-NoValue-)\leqslant\log\left({\frac{\tr\left({P}\right)}{\tr\left({P\rho^{\prime}}\right)}}\right)=\log\left({\frac{\tr\left({Q}\right)}{\tr\left({Q\rho}\right)}}\right)\leqslant H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)+\zeta\ .

(224)

For all
j\in\{{1,2,\ldots,n-1}\}
, we
write, for short,
A_{j}\coloneqq S_{1}S_{2}\ldots S_{j}
and
B_{j+1}\coloneqq S_{j+1}S_{j+2}\ldots S_{n}
.
Since every POVM effect in
\mathsf{M}^{r=0}
is tensor-product,
Section

D.2
implies that, for all
j
,

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r=0},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho^{\prime}-NoValue-)=-D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r=0},\,{\eta}}(\rho^{\prime}\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\mathds{1}_{S})\geqslant-D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}(\rho^{\prime}_{A_{j}}\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\mathds{1}_{A_{j}})-D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}(\rho^{\prime}_{B_{j+1}}\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\mathds{1}_{B_{j+1}})\ .

(225)

Consequently,

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r=0},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho^{\prime}-NoValue-)\geqslant\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\left[{-D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}(\rho^{\prime}_{A_{j}}\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\mathds{1}_{A_{j}})-D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}(\rho^{\prime}_{B_{j+1}}\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\mathds{1}_{B_{j+1}})}\right]\ .

(226)

By Proposition 4.67 of Ref.
Khatri and Wilde 2020
, one can bound each hypothesis-testing relative entropy in
(
226
)
in terms of a standard (Umegaki) quantum relative entropy: for each
A_{j}
,

\displaystyle-D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}(\rho^{\prime}_{A_{j}}\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\mathds{1}_{A_{j}})

\displaystyle\geqslant-\frac{1}{\eta}\left[{D(\rho^{\prime}_{A_{j}}\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\mathds{1}_{A_{j}})+h(\eta)+({1-\eta})\log\bm{(}\tr({\mathds{1}_{A_{j}}})\bm{)}}\right]-\log(\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\eta}\left[{H\big({\rho^{\prime}_{A_{j}}}\big)-h(\eta)-j(1-\eta)\log(2)}\right]-\log(\eta)\ .

(227)

Similarly, for each
B_{j+1}
,

\displaystyle-D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}(\rho^{\prime}_{B_{j+1}}\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\mathds{1}_{B_{j+1}})

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{\eta}\left[{H\big({\rho^{\prime}_{B_{j+1}}}\big)-h(\eta)-(n-j)({1-\eta})\log(2)}\right]-\log(\eta)\ .

(228)

Thus,

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)+\zeta

\displaystyle\geqslant H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r=0},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho^{\prime}-NoValue-)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\left\{{\frac{1}{\eta}\left[{H\big({\rho^{\prime}_{A_{j}}}\big)+H\big({\rho^{\prime}_{B_{j+1}}}\big)-2h(\eta)-({1-\eta})\,n\log(2)}\right]-2\log(\eta)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\eta}\left\{{\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\left[{I(A_{j}\,:\,B_{j+1})_{\rho^{\prime}}+H\left({\rho^{\prime}}\right)}\right]-2h(\eta)-({1-\eta})\,n\log(2)}\right\}-2\log(\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\eta}\left[{E(\rho^{\prime})+H(\rho^{\prime})-2h(\eta)-({1-\eta})\,n\log(2)}\right]-2\log(\eta)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{\eta}\left[{E(\rho)-r\mu(\mathcal{G})+H(\rho)-2h(\eta)-({1-\eta})\,n\log(2)}\right]-2\log(\eta)\ .

(229)

The first and second equalities follow from the definitions
(
209
)
and
(
210
)
, respectively.
The last inequality follows because
E(\rho^{\prime})-E(\rho)\geqslant-r\mu(\mathcal{G})
, by
Appendix

G
, and because
H(\rho^{\prime})=H(\rho)
, since the von Neumann entropy is unitarily invariant.
Finally,
(
229
)
implies
(
223
)
, since
\zeta
is arbitrary.
∎

Appendix H
Bounds from short-time Hamiltonian dynamics

Here, we apply the bounds in
Appendix

G
to short-time Hamiltonian dynamics.
We consider a nearest-neighbor Hamiltonian
H
acting on a 1D chain
S
of
n\geqslant 2
qubits.
The chain is initially in a state
\rho
.
We show that the entanglement measure
E(\rho)
can grow only slowly in time.

Corollary 57

(Continuity bound on
E(\rho)
under local Hamiltonian evolution)
.

Let
\rho
denote any quantum state of
S
.
Let
H
denote a nearest-neighbor, translationally invariant Hamiltonian governed by local-interaction terms
h
that can act on only two qubits at a time.
It holds that

\frac{d}{dt}\left\lvert{E\left({e^{-iHt}\rho e^{iHt}}\right)-\rho}\right\rvert\leqslant C(n-1)\lVert{h}\rVert\ ,

(230)

for some universal constant
C>0
.

Proof.
The Hamiltonian has the form

H=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\tau_{j}(h)\ ,

(231)

wherein
h
denotes a fixed interaction term that acts nontrivially only on two neighboring qubits in
S
.
For all
j\in\left\{{1,2,\ldots,n-1}\right\}
,
\tau_{j}
is the shift operator that places
\tau_{j}(h)
onto qubits
j
and
j+1
.
By the Trotter formula, it is manifest that for suitably small times
t>0
,
e^{-iHt}\rho e^{iHt}
can be arbitrarily well approximated by a brickwork circuit involving nearest-neighbor quantum gates, even for noncommuting
\tau_{j}(h)
for different
j
.
For each cut specified by
j
, the small incremental entangling bound for the von-Neumann entropy
Mariën et al. 2016
;
Eisert 2021
gives

\frac{d}{dt}I(S_{1}\ldots S_{j}:S_{j+1}\ldots S_{n})_{\rho}\leqslant C\lVert{h}\rVert\ .

(232)

Summing this inequality over all cuts yields
(
230
)
.
The constant
C
is proven in Ref.
Mariën et al. 2016
to be
C=22\log(2)
.
The
2
originates from the qubits’ local dimension.
In numerical studies, it is found to be rather tightly given by
C=22\log(2)
.
∎

The linear growth of
E(\rho)
in time is tight, in the sense that there exist local nearest-neighbor Hamiltonians that exhibit such growth for suitably short times.
This claim largely follows from the results of Ref.
Schuch et al. 2008
, which build on those in Ref.
Cramer et al. 2008
.
Intuitively speaking, the linear growth originates from a light-cone-like entanglement dynamics governed by Lieb-Robinson bounds.
For free bosons and fermions (called noninteracting bosons and fermions in this context), such bounds are readily computable.

Corollary 58

(Lower bound on
E(\rho)
under local Hamiltonian evolution)
.

Let
\rho
denote any quantum state of
S
.
There exist local Hamiltonians and product initial conditions
\rho
, with
E(\rho)=0
, such that

E\left({e^{-iHt}\rho e^{iHt}}\right)\geqslant(n-1)\left[{\frac{4}{3\pi}-\frac{1}{2}\log(t)-1}\right]\ .

(233)

Proof.
Let
S
consist of an odd number
n\geqslant 21
of qubits.
Let
H
be the Ising Hamiltonian with periodic boundary conditions.
Let the initial state be
\rho=\lvert{1,1,\ldots,1}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{1,1,\ldots,1}\rvert
, so that
E(\rho)=0
.
The time-evolved state is
\rho(t)=e^{-iHt}\rho e^{iHt}
.
For this state, bounds on entanglement entropies for sufficiently large subsystems are known.
Since we consider only bipartite cuts of
S
, the subsystem of length
L
considered in Ref.
Schuch et al. 2008
always satisfies
L=(n-1)/2
.
Hence, the constraint
L\geqslant 10
is always satisfied.
It follows from Theorem 1 in Ref.
Schuch et al. 2008
, applied to the
n-1
cuts of
S
, that

E\bm{(}\rho(t)\bm{)}\geqslant(n-1)\left[{\frac{4}{3\pi}-\frac{1}{2}\log(t)-1}\right]\ .

(234)

∎

Appendix J
Recovering the hypothesis-testing (relative) entropy at high complexities

Consider an “unrestricted” agent who can render POVM effects of arbitrarily high complexities.
In this Appendix, we formulate conditions under which the complexity entropy approximates the hypothesis-testing entropy.
We first identify the set of measurements that an unrestricted agent can render with arbitrary precision.

Definition 59

(POVM effects accessible with unbounded complexity)
.

Let
\{\mathsf{M}^{r}\}
denote any family of POVM-effect-complexity sets (
Section

B.3
).
The set of
POVM effects accessible with unbounded complexity
is

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}^{\infty}\coloneqq\overline{\bigcup_{r\geqslant 0}\mathsf{M}^{r}}\ .

(235)

The
\overline{A}
denotes the topological closure of a set
A
.

Definition 60

(“Complexity-unrestricted” entropy and relative entropy)
.

Let
\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
denote any set of POVM effects defined by
(
235
)
.
Let
\rho
denote any subnormalized state; and
\Gamma
, any positive-semidefinite operator.
Let
\eta\in(0,\tr(\rho)]
.
We define the “complexity-unrestricted” relative entropy
D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{\infty},\,{\eta}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)
by replacing
\mathsf{M}^{r}
with
\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
in the definition of
D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)
(
Section

D.1
).
The “complexity-unrestricted” entropy is
H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{\infty},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)\coloneqq-D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{\infty},\,{\eta}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\mathds{1})
.

If
\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
contains all POVM effects, we recover the hypothesis-testing (relative) entropy exactly:

H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{\infty},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)=H_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)

[{D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{\infty},\,{\eta}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)=D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)}]
.
Yet, for many natural families
\{\mathsf{M}^{r}\}
of POVM-effect-complexity sets,
\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
contains only a limited portion of all POVM effects.
For instance, let
C
denote a unitary-complexity measure (
Section

B.1
), and let
\mathsf{P}
denote a set of simple POVM effects (
Section

B.4
) consisting only of projectors.
Suppose that
\left\{{\mathsf{M}^{r}}\right\}=\left\{{\mathsf{M}^{r}(\mathsf{P},C)}\right\}
, as per
(
87
)
.
Since every unitary transformation maps projectors to projectors,
\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
is a set of projectors and therefore excludes almost all POVM effects.

However, even when limited in form,
\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
may contain enough effects to ensure that
H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)
converges to a value
near

H_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)
as
r\to\infty
.
We now formulate conditions under which
H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{\infty},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)

[{D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{\infty},\,{\eta}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)}]
lies close to the hypothesis-testing (relative) entropy,
H_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)

[{D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)}]
.

Definition 61

(
q
-quasiuniversal sets of POVM effects)
.

Let
q\geqslant 0
, and let
\Gamma
denote any positive-semidefinite operator.
Let
\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
denote any set of POVM effects defined by
(
235
)
.

\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
is
q
-quasiuniversal with respect to
\Gamma
if, for all POVM effects
Q
with
\lVert{Q}\rVert=1
and for all probability weights
g\in(0,1)
, there exist accessible effects
\tilde{Q},\tilde{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
such that

\displaystyle g\tilde{Q}\leqslant Q\leqslant(1-g)\tilde{Q}^{\prime}+g\mathds{1}\

(236a)

\displaystyle\text{and }\hskip 1.42271pt\log\bm{(}\tr({\tilde{Q}^{\prime}\Gamma})\bm{)}-\log\bm{(}\tr({\tilde{Q}\Gamma})\bm{)}\leqslant q\ .

(236b)

Consider any
Q
with
\lVert{Q}\rVert=1
.
Whenever
\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
is quasiuniversal (for any
q
), one can “underestimate”
Q
by forming a convex mixture from an accessible effect
\tilde{Q}\in\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
and the trivial effect
0
.
Similarly, one can “overestimate”
Q
by mixing an accessible
\tilde{Q}^{\prime}
and the trivial effect
\mathds{1}
.
One can estimate
Q
with such mixtures, no matter the mixing probability
g
.
The variable
g
continuously parametrizes inequalities that approach
0\leqslant Q\leqslant\tilde{Q}^{\prime}
as
g\to 0
and
\tilde{Q}\leqslant Q\leqslant\mathds{1}
as
g\to 1
.
These boundaries provide intuition about the parametrization: as one requirement (that
\tilde{Q}
lower-bound
Q
) tightens, the other requirement (that
\tilde{Q}^{\prime}
upper-bound
Q
) relaxes and vice versa.
We exclude the probability weight
g=0
(
g=1
) to avoid trivializing the role of
\tilde{Q}
(
\tilde{Q}^{\prime})
.

The estimations
\tilde{Q}
and
\tilde{Q}^{\prime}
lie close to
Q
because they lie close to one another, by condition
(
236b
)
:
their candidate hypothesis-testing-entropy values
\log\bm{(}\tr({\tilde{Q}\Gamma})/\eta\bm{)}
and
\log\bm{(}\tr({\tilde{Q}^{\prime}\Gamma})/\eta\bm{)}
differ by
\leqslant q
for every error intolerance
\eta\in(0,1]
.
Without
(
236b
)
, one might trivially satisfy
(
236a
)
with
\tilde{Q}=0
and
\tilde{Q}^{\prime}=\mathds{1}
.
Finally, as expected,
\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
is
q
-quasiuniversal for every
q
and with respect to every
\Gamma
, whenever the set contains all POVM effects.
In this case, one can choose
\tilde{Q}=\tilde{Q}^{\prime}=Q\in\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
.

The hypothesis-testing (relative) entropy bounds the complexity-unrestricted (relative) entropy from above and below.

Theorem 62

(Recoverability of the hypothesis-testing entropy)
.

Let
\rho
denote any subnormalized state; and
\Gamma
, any positive-semidefinite operator.
Let
\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
denote any set of POVM effects defined by
(
235
)
.
Let
q\geqslant 0
,
\eta\in(0,\tr(\rho)]
, and
g\in(0,1)
.
Suppose that
\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
is
q
-quasiuniversal with respect to
\Gamma
(
Appendix

J
).
Then

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)\geqslant D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{\infty},\,{\eta}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)\geqslant D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta_{g}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)-q-\log\left({\frac{\tr({\rho})}{g\eta}}\right)\ ,

(237)

wherein
\eta_{g}\coloneqq(1-g)\eta+g\tr({\rho})\geqslant\eta
.
If
\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
is
q
-quasiuniversal with respect to
\mathds{1}
, then

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)\leqslant H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{\infty},\,{\eta}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)\leqslant H_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta_{g}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)+q+\log\left({\frac{\tr({\rho})}{g\eta}}\right)\ .

(238)

Proof.

The
D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)
optimization ranges over all POVM effects, while the
D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{\infty},\,{\eta}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)
optimization ranges over only effects in
\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
.
Therefore,
D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)\geqslant D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{\infty},\,{\eta}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)
.
Now, we prove the second inequality in
(
237
)
.
Let
Q
denote an optimal POVM effect for
D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta_{g}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)
in
(
93
)
.
Without loss of generality,
\lVert{Q}\rVert=1
, since we could otherwise replace
Q
by another candidate
Q^{\prime}\coloneqq Q/\lVert{Q}\rVert\geqslant Q
that achieves the same objective value as
Q
.
Moreover,
\tr({Q\rho})\geqslant\eta_{g}
and
\tr({Q\Gamma})/\tr({Q\rho})=\exp\bm{(}-D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta_{g}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)\bm{)}
.
Since
\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
is
q
-quasiuniversal, some
\tilde{Q},\tilde{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
satisfy the conditions
(
236
)
.
Condition
(
236a
)
implies that

\displaystyle(1-g)\tr({\tilde{Q}^{\prime}\rho})\geqslant\tr({Q\rho})-g\tr({\rho})\geqslant\eta_{g}-g\tr({\rho})=(1-g)\eta\ .

(239)

Equivalently,
\tr({\tilde{Q}^{\prime}\rho})\geqslant\eta
.
Therefore,
\tilde{Q}^{\prime}
is a candidate for the
D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{\infty},\,{\eta}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)
optimization in
(
99
)
, so

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{\infty},\,{\eta}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)

\displaystyle\geqslant-\log\left({\frac{\tr\left({\tilde{Q}^{\prime}\Gamma}\right)}{\tr\left({\tilde{Q}^{\prime}\rho}\right)}}\right)\geqslant-\log\left({\frac{\tr\left({\tilde{Q}^{\prime}\Gamma}\right)}{\eta}}\right)\geqslant-\log\left({e^{q}\frac{\tr\left({\tilde{Q}\Gamma}\right)}{\eta}}\right)\geqslant-\log\left({\frac{\tr\left({Q\Gamma}\right)}{g\eta}}\right)-q

\displaystyle\geqslant-\log\left({\frac{\tr\left({Q\Gamma}\right)}{\tr\left({Q\rho}\right)}\;\frac{\tr\left({\rho}\right)}{g\eta}}\right)-q=D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\eta_{g}}(\rho\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\Gamma)-q-\log\left({\frac{\tr\left({\rho}\right)}{g\eta}}\right)\ .

(240)

The third inequality holds because
\tr({\tilde{Q}^{\prime}\Gamma})\leqslant e^{q}\tr({\tilde{Q}\Gamma})
, by condition
(
236b
)
.
The fourth inequality follows from condition
(
236a
)
;
the fifth from
\tr({Q\rho})\leqslant\tr({\rho})
.
∎

We now provide an example of quasiuniversal set that arises naturally in quantum computation.

Proposition 63

(Example of
q
-quasiuniversal set)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
denote any set of unitary gates that is universal for quantum computation.
Let
C_{\mathcal{G}}
be the unitary-circuit-complexity measure associated with
\mathcal{G}
(
Section

B.1
).
Let
\mathsf{P}
denote a set of simple POVM effects (
Section

B.4
) that consists only of projectors and contains at least one rank-1 projector.
Let
\left\{{\mathsf{M}^{r}=\mathsf{M}^{r}\left({\mathsf{P},C_{\mathcal{G}}}\right)}\right\}
denote the family of POVM-effect-complexity sets defined by
(
87
)
.
Let
\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
denote the set of POVM effects defined in
(
235
)
, and assume that the effects in
\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
act on a Hilbert space of dimensionality
d
.

\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
is
q
-quasiuniversal with respect to
\mathds{1}
(
Appendix

J
).
The
q
denotes the largest gap within the set
\mathcal{X}\coloneqq\left\{{\log\bm{(}\tr(P)\bm{)}:P\in\mathsf{P}}\right\}\subset[0,\log(d)]
:

\displaystyle q\coloneqq\sup\left\{{\lvert{b-a}\rvert:(a,b)\subset[0,\log(d)]\setminus\mathcal{X}}\right\}\ .

(241)

q
is the greatest length of any interval
(a,b)\subset[0,\log(d)]
disjoint from
\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

Let
Q
denote any POVM effect with
\lVert{Q}\rVert=1
.
Let us fix a matrix representation of
Q
that is diagonal and has decreasing diagonal elements:
Q=\diag({\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{d}})
, with
1=\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\ldots\geqslant\lambda_{d}\geqslant 0
.
Consider any
g\in(0,1)
, and let
\ell_{g}\coloneqq\max\{{i:\lambda_{i}\geqslant g}\}
denote the number of
Q
eigenvalues not less than
g
.

\ell_{g}
satisfies
1\leqslant\ell_{g}\leqslant d
, since
\lambda_{1}=1\geqslant g
.
Let

\displaystyle\ell_{g}^{-}\coloneqq\max\left\{{\rank(P)\,:\;P\in\mathsf{P},\ \rank(P)\leqslant\ell_{g}}\right\}\;\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;\ell_{g}^{+}\coloneqq\min\left\{{\rank(P)\,:\;P\in\mathsf{P},\ \rank(P)\geqslant\ell_{g}}\right\}\ .

(242)

As per the definitions of
\ell_{g}^{-}
and
\ell_{g}^{+}
, there exist projectors
\tilde{P},\tilde{P}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}
such that
\rank(\tilde{P})=\ell_{g}^{-}
and
\rank(\tilde{P}^{\prime})=\ell_{g}^{+}
.
Moreover,
1\leqslant\ell_{g}^{-}\leqslant\ell_{g}^{+}\leqslant d
, since
\mathsf{P}
contains both a rank-1 projector and
\mathds{1}
.
Let
U
denote a unitary such that
\tilde{Q}=U^{\dagger}\,\tilde{P}\,U
is diagonal:
\tilde{Q}=\diag(1,\ldots,1,0,\ldots,0)
, with
\rank(\tilde{Q})=\ell_{g}^{-}
.
Likewise, let
U^{\prime}
denote a unitary such that
\tilde{Q}=U^{\prime\dagger}\,\tilde{P}^{\prime}\,U^{\prime}
is diagonal, with
\rank(\tilde{Q}^{\prime})=\ell_{g}^{+}
.
By assumption,
U
and
U^{\prime}
are arbitrarily well-approximated by unitaries of finite complexity, so
\tilde{Q},\tilde{Q}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{M}^{\infty}
.
We now show that
\tilde{Q}
and
\tilde{Q}^{\prime}
satisfy the conditions
(
236
)
in the definition of a
q
-quasiuniversal set.

Consider the operator
g\tilde{Q}
.
The first
\ell_{g}\geqslant\ell_{g}^{-}
diagonal entries of
g\tilde{Q}
do not exceed
g
and, hence, do not exceed the first
\ell_{g}
diagonal entries of
Q
.
Moreover, the last
\ell_{g}
diagonal entries of
g\tilde{Q}
equal 0 and, hence, do not exceed the last
\ell_{g}
diagonal entries of
Q
.
Thus,
g\tilde{Q}\leqslant Q
.
Now, consider the operator
(1-g)\tilde{Q}^{\prime}+g\mathds{1}
.
The first
\ell_{g}\leqslant\ell_{g}^{+}
diagonal entries of
(1-g)\tilde{Q}^{\prime}+g\mathds{1}
equal 1 and, hence, are not less than the first
\ell_{g}
diagonal entries of
Q
.
Moreover, the last
\ell_{g}
diagonal entries of
g\tilde{Q}
are not less than
g
and, hence, are not less than the last
\ell_{g}
diagonal entries of
Q
.
Thus,
Q\leqslant(1-g)\tilde{Q}^{\prime}+g\mathds{1}
.
As such,
\tilde{Q}
and
\tilde{Q}^{\prime}
satisfy condition
(
236a
)
.

Compare the definitions of
\ell_{g}^{-}
and
\ell_{g}^{+}
.
There exists no projector
P\in\mathsf{P}
such that
\ell_{g}^{-}<\rank(P)<\ell_{g}^{+}
.
Accordingly, the interval
\bm{(}\log({\ell_{g}^{-}}),\log({\ell_{g}^{+}})\bm{)}
is disjoint from
\mathcal{X}
.

[
If
\ell_{g}^{-}=\ell_{g}^{+}
, then
\bm{(}\log({\ell_{g}^{-}}),\log({\ell_{g}^{+}})\bm{)}=\emptyset
by convention.
]

Thus,
\log\bm{(}\tr({\tilde{Q}^{\prime}})\bm{)}-\log\bm{(}\tr({\tilde{Q}})\bm{)}=\log({\ell_{g}^{+}})-\log({\ell_{g}^{-}})\leqslant q
, with
q
defined as in
(
241
)
, so
\tilde{Q}
and
\tilde{Q}^{\prime}
satisfy condition
(
236b
)
.
∎

Appendix K
Data compression under complexity limitations

Here, we establish optimal protocols for data compression under complexity limitations.
We thereby prove the bounds
(
50
)
in
Section

V.5
.

Let
\rho
denote any state, and
C
any unitary-complexity measure, of
n
qubits.
Let
r\geqslant 0
.
Let
m\leqslant n
denote a number of qubits; and
\epsilon\in[0,1)
, an error parameter.
We define an
(m,r,\epsilon)
–data-compression protocol
as a unitary
U
that satisfies
C(U)\leqslant r
and that can compress
\rho
into
m
qubits.
The compression succeeds if the other
n-m
qubits end in a state
\epsilon
-close to
\lvert{0^{n-m}}\rangle
in fidelity:
there exists a subset
\mathcal{W}\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,n}\}
of qubits such that
\lvert{\mathcal{W}}\rvert=m
and

\displaystyle\tr\bm{(}\tr_{\mathcal{W}}(U\rho U^{\dagger})\,\lvert{0^{n-m}}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{0^{n-m}}\rvert\bm{)}\geqslant 1-\epsilon\ .

(243)

We first prove that some protocol compresses
\rho
into a number of qubits proportional to the reduced complexity entropy (
Section

D.5.1
).

Theorem 64

(Achievability of data compression)
.

Let
\rho
denote any state, and
C
any unitary-complexity measure (
Section

B.1
), of
n
qubits.
Let
\mathsf{P}
denote the set of simple POVM effects (
Section

B.4
) defined in
(
10
)
.
Let
r\geqslant 0
and
\epsilon\in[0,1)
.
Let
\mathsf{M}^{r}=\mathsf{M}^{r}(\mathsf{P},C)
denote the POVM-effect-complexity set defined in
(
87
)
.
There exists an
(m,r,\epsilon)
–data-compression protocol for
\rho
such that

\displaystyle m=\frac{1}{\log(2)}\,H_{\mathrm{h}}^{{r},\,{1-\epsilon}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)\ .

(244)

The reduced complexity entropy
H_{\mathrm{h}}^{{r},\,{1-\epsilon}}
is defined with respect to
\mathsf{M}^{r}
.

The above theorem can be reformulated in terms of the complexity entropy
H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{1-\epsilon}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)
.

Section

D.5.1
shows that the reduced complexity entropy
H_{\mathrm{h}}^{{r},\,{1-\epsilon}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)
differs from the complexity entropy
H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{1-\epsilon}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)
by at most
\log(1/[1-\epsilon])
.
Combining this fact with
Appendix

K
yields the bounds
(
50
)
in
Section

V.5
.
If
\epsilon\approx 0
, then the optimal number of qubits in
(
244
)
is approximately proportional to the complexity entropy:
m\approx H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{1-\epsilon}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)/\log(2)
.

We further prove that the protocol in
Appendix

K
is optimal.

Theorem 65

(Optimality of data compression)
.

Let
\rho
denote any state of
n
qubits.
Let
r\geqslant 0
and
\epsilon\in[0,1)
.
Let
\mathsf{M}^{r}
denote a POVM-effect-complexity set as in
Appendix

K
.
Every
(m,r,\epsilon)
–data-compression protocol satisfies

\displaystyle m\geqslant\frac{1}{\log(2)}\,H_{\mathrm{h}}^{{r},\,{1-\epsilon}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)\ .

(245)

The reduced complexity entropy
H_{\mathrm{h}}^{{r},\,{1-\epsilon}}
is defined with respect to
\mathsf{M}^{r}
.

The above data-compression problem maps directly onto the thermodynamic-erasure problem in
Section

III.1
.
The proofs of
Appendices

K
and
K
are closely related to the arguments in
Section

III.1
and to the proof of
Appendix

E
.

Proof of
Appendix K
.

Consider any
\zeta>0
.
There exists a
Q\in\mathsf{M}^{r}
such that
\tr({Q\rho})\geqslant 1-\epsilon
and
\log\bm{(}\tr({Q})\bm{)}\leqslant H_{\mathrm{h}}^{{r},\,{1-\epsilon}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)+\zeta
.

Q=U^{\dagger}PU
for some unitary
U
satisfying
C(U)\leqslant r
and for some simple POVM effect
P
acting as
\mathds{1}_{2}
on every qubit in a subset
\mathcal{W}\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,n}\}
and as
\lvert{0}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{0}\rvert
on every other qubit.
Without loss of generality, assume that
\mathcal{W}
consists of the first
m\coloneqq\lvert{\mathcal{W}}\rvert
of the
n
qubits.
Hence
P=\mathds{1}_{2}^{\otimes m}\otimes\lvert{0^{n-m}}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{0^{n-m}}\rvert
.

U
is an
(m,r,\epsilon)
–data-compression protocol for
\rho
, since
C(U)\leqslant r
and condition
(
243
)
holds:

\displaystyle\tr\bm{(}\tr_{\mathcal{W}}(U\rho U^{\dagger})\,\lvert{0^{n-m}}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{0^{n-m}}\rvert\bm{)}=\tr([U\rho U^{\dagger}]\,[\mathds{1}_{2}^{\otimes m}\otimes\lvert{0^{n-m}}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{0^{n-m}}\rvert])=\tr([U\rho U^{\dagger}]\,P)=\tr({Q\rho})

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-\epsilon\ .

(246)

Moreover,

\displaystyle m=\log_{2}\bm{(}\tr({P})\bm{)}=\log_{2}\bm{(}\tr({Q})\bm{)}=\frac{1}{\log(2)}\,\log\bm{(}\tr({Q})\bm{)}\leqslant\frac{1}{\log(2)}\left(H_{\mathrm{h}}^{{r},\,{1-\epsilon}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)+\zeta\right)\ ,

(247)

which implies
(
244
)
, since
\zeta
is arbitrary.
∎

Proof of
Appendix K
.

Let
U
denote any
(m,r,\epsilon)
–data-compression protocol for
\rho
.
By definition, there exists a subset
\mathcal{W}\subset\{{1,2,\ldots,n}\}
such that
\lvert{\mathcal{W}}\rvert=m
and condition
(
243
)
holds.
Without loss of generality, assume that
\mathcal{W}
consists of the first
m
of
n
qubits.
Let
P\coloneqq\mathds{1}_{2}^{\otimes m}\otimes\lvert{0^{n-m}}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{0^{n-m}}\rvert
and
Q\coloneqq U^{\dagger}PU
.
By construction,
Q
is in
\mathsf{M}^{r}
and satisfies

\displaystyle\tr({Q\rho})=\tr({P\,U\rho U^{\dagger}})=\tr\bm{(}\tr_{\mathcal{W}}({U\rho U^{\dagger}})\,\lvert{0^{n-m}}\rangle\mkern-1.8mu\langle{0^{n-m}}\rvert\bm{)}\geqslant 1-\epsilon\ .

(248)

Q
is therefore a candidate for the
H_{\mathrm{h}}^{{r},\,{1-\epsilon}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)
optimization, so

\displaystyle m=\lvert{\mathcal{W}}\rvert=\log_{2}\bm{(}\tr({P})\bm{)}=\log_{2}\bm{(}\tr({Q})\bm{)}\geqslant\frac{1}{\log(2)}H_{\mathrm{h}}^{{r},\,{1-\epsilon}}-NoValue-(\rho -NoValue-)\ .

(249)

∎

Appendix L
Decoupling from a reference system

Here, we prove the decoupling bound of
Section

V.7
.
Our proof relies on the following conjectured property of the complexity conditional entropy.

Conjecture 66
.

Let
A
,
B
, and
R
denote distinct quantum systems.
Let
\rho_{ABR}
denote any state of
ABR
.
Let
r\geqslant 0
and
\eta\in(0,1]
.
It holds that

\displaystyle H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(B\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho}\leqslant H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(AB\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho}+\log(d_{A})\ .

(250)

Equivalently,

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(\rho_{BR}\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\pi_{B}\otimes\rho_{R})\geqslant D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(\rho_{ABR}\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\pi_{AB}\otimes\rho_{R})-2\log(d_{A})\ .

(251)

The equivalence between
(
250
)
and
(
251
)
holds because
H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(B\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho}=\log(d_{B})-D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(\rho_{BR}\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\pi_{B}\otimes\rho_{R})
and because
H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(AB\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho}=\log(d_{A}d_{B})-D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r},\,{\eta}}(\rho_{ABR}\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\pi_{AB}\otimes\rho_{R})
, by the argument in
(
176
)
.

(
250
)
mirrors a known inequality for the von Neumann conditional entropy
H(AB\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho}
.
The latter inequality follows from a chain rule and the lower bound
H(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}BR)_{\rho}\geqslant-\log(d_{A})
:

\displaystyle H(B\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho}=H(AB\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho}-H(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}BR)_{\rho}\leqslant H(AB\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho}+\log(d_{A})\ .

(252)

Theorem 67

(Upper bound on the number of qubits Alice can decouple under complexity limitations)
.

Let
A
denote an
n
-qubit system, and let
R
denote a quantum system distinct from
A
.
Let
\rho_{AR}
denote any state of
AR
.
Let
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{P}_{AR}
denote any set of simple POVM effects (
Section

B.4
); and
C=C_{AR}
, any adjoint-invariant unitary-complexity measure (
Section

B.1
).
Let
\left\{{\mathsf{M}^{r}=\mathsf{M}^{r}(\mathsf{P},C)}\right\}
denote the family of POVM-effect-complexity sets defined by
(
87
)
.
Let
r_{0}\geqslant 0
and
r_{1}\geqslant r_{0}
.
Let
\eta\in(0,1]
and
\delta\in(0,1]
.
Let
U_{0}
denote any unitary on
A
satisfying
C(U_{0})\leqslant r_{0}
.
Let
A_{1}
denote a subsystem of
k\geqslant 0
qubits in
A
; and
A_{2}
, the subsystem of the other
n-k
qubits.
Let

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}_{AR}

\displaystyle\coloneqq(U_{0}\otimes\mathds{1}_{R})\rho_{AR}(U_{0}\otimes\mathds{1}_{R})^{\dagger}\ .

(253)

Suppose that

\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r_{1}},\,{\eta}}(\rho^{\prime}_{A_{2}R}\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\pi_{A_{2}}\otimes\rho_{R})\leqslant-\log\left(\frac{\delta}{\eta}\right)\ ,

(254)

wherein
D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r_{1}},\,{\eta}}
is defined with respect to
\mathsf{M}^{r_{1}}
.
Assume
Appendix

L
holds.
It holds that

\displaystyle k\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\left[n-\frac{1}{\log(2)}H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r_{1}-r_{0}},\,{\eta}}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho}+\log_{2}\left(\frac{\delta}{\eta}\right)\right]\ ,

(255)

wherein
H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r_{1}-r_{0}},\,{\eta}}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)
is defined with respect to
\mathsf{M}^{r_{1}-r_{0}}
.

Proof.

The following chain of inequalities implies
(
255
)
:

\displaystyle-\log\left(\frac{\delta}{\eta}\right)

\displaystyle\geqslant D_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r_{1}},\,{\eta}}(\rho^{\prime}_{A_{2}R}\mathclose{}\,\|\,\mathopen{}\pi_{A_{2}}\otimes\rho_{R})

\displaystyle=\log\left(d_{A_{2}}\right)-H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r_{1}},\,{\eta}}(A_{2}\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\log\left(d_{A_{2}}\right)-H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r_{1}},\,{\eta}}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho^{\prime}}-\log\left(d_{A_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=(n-2k)\log(2)-H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r_{1}},\,{\eta}}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\geqslant(n-2k)\log(2)-H_{\mathrm{H}}^{{r_{1}-r_{0}},\,{\eta}}(A\mathclose{}\,|\,\mathopen{}R)_{\rho}\ .

(256)

The first equality follows by the argument in
(
176
)
; the second inequality, by the assumption that
Appendix

L
holds; and the last inequality, by
Section

D.2
.
∎
